<!-- assumption-1: body_md -->
\label{asmpt:set}
The constraint set $\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^d$ is bounded, closed, convex, and has nonempty interior. In addition, we assume $0\in\mathcal{C}$ and $\mathcal{C}\subset B(0,R)$ for some $R>0$.
<!-- end assumption-1 -->
<!-- assumption-2: body_md -->
\label{asmpt:continuous:regularity}
In addition to Assumption~\ref{asmpt:set}, suppose that $\partial\mathcal C$ is $C^2$ or $C^{1,1}$, that $U-U_0$ is continuous on $\overline{\mathcal C}$, and that $U_0\in C^2(\overline{\mathcal C})$.
<!-- end assumption-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:reflected:timechange}
Under Assumption~\ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity}, the normally reflected martingale problem with Neumann boundary condition for
$$
\mathcal Lf=e^{U-U_0}\bigl(\Delta f-\langle\nabla U_0,\nabla f\rangle\bigr)
$$
is well posed for every initial point in $\mathcal C$. Write $(P_t)_{t\geq0}$ for its transition semigroup.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{proof:lem:reflected:timechange}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:continuous:structure}
Under Assumption~\ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity}, let $(P_t)_{t\geq0}$ be the transition semigroup from Lemma~\ref{lem:reflected:timechange}, and write $Z:=\int_{\mathcal C}e^{-U(x)}dx$ and $\pi(dx):=Z^{-1}e^{-U(x)}\mathbf1_{\mathcal C}(x)dx$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item For $f\in C^2(\overline{\mathcal C})$ satisfying the Neumann condition
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:boundary}
\nabla f\cdot\nu=0\quad\text{on }\partial\mathcal C,
\end{equation}
the generator acts as
\begin{equation}\label{eqn:generator}
\mathcal Lf=e^{U-U_0}\bigl(\Delta f-\langle\nabla U_0,\nabla f\rangle\bigr).
\end{equation}
\item The form
$$
\mathcal E(f,g):=\frac1Z\int_{\mathcal C}e^{-U_0(x)}\langle\nabla f(x),\nabla g(x)\rangle dx,\qquad \mathcal D(\mathcal E)=H^1(\mathcal C),
$$
is a densely defined closed symmetric Markov form on $L^2(\pi)$ whose semigroup is $P_t$. Its nonpositive self adjoint generator agrees with $\mathcal L$ on the Neumann test functions in (i). Moreover, $P_t1=1$ and, for every $f\in L^2(\pi)$ and $t>0$,
$$
P_tf\in\mathcal D(\mathcal L),\qquad
\frac{d}{dt}\|P_tf\|_{L^2(\pi)}^2=-2\mathcal E(P_tf,P_tf).
$$
\item The semigroup $P_t$ admits a jointly continuous, strictly positive Neumann heat kernel $\kappa_t(x,y)$ relative to $\pi$ on $(0,\infty)\times\mathcal C\times\mathcal C$, including boundary points. The Lebesgue transition density $p(t,x,y)=\kappa_t(x,y)Z^{-1}e^{-U(y)}$ has the same continuity and positivity properties. In particular, for every $s>0$,
$$
0<\min_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_s(x,y)\leq\max_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_s(x,y)<\infty.
$$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:continuous:structure}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:Gibbs}
Under Assumption~\ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity}, the Gibbs distribution $\pi(dx)\propto e^{-U(x)}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal C}(x)\,dx$ is the unique invariant distribution of \eqref{eqn:RALD}.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:thm:Gibbs}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:chi:square}
Under Assumption~\ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity}, assume that $\mu_0\ll\pi$ and $\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)<\infty$. If $\pi$ satisfies the Poincar\'{e} inequality
$$
    \operatorname{Var}_{\pi}(f)
    \leq
    C_P\int_{\mathcal C}\|\nabla f\|^2\,d\pi
$$
for every $f\in H^1(\mathcal C)$, \footnote{This is the standard spectral gap form of the Poincar\'e inequality. See e.g. \cite{Bakry2008,Bakry2013}.}then
\begin{equation}
	\chi^{2}(\mu_{t}\Vert\pi)\leq
	\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\Vert\pi)e^{-2\alpha_{0} t/C_{P}},
\end{equation}
provided that $\alpha_{0}:=e^{\inf_{x\in\mathcal{C}}(U(x)-U_{0}(x))}>0$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:thm:chi:square}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:W:2}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}, assume that $\mathcal{C}$ is contained in a ball with radius $R>0$ and centered at zero. Then, we have
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{t},\pi)\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{0} t/(2C_{P})},
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_{0}$ and $C_{P}$ are defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:cor:W:2}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- assumption-3: body_md -->
\label{asmpt:strongly:convex}
There exists $m>0$ such that:
\begin{equation}
\langle b(x)-b(y),\,x-y\rangle \leq -m\|x-y\|^2,
\qquad \text{for any $x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}$}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-3 -->
<!-- assumption-4: body_md -->
\label{asmpt:smooth}
The drift $b:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{R}^d$ and the scalar diffusion coefficient $\sigma:\mathcal{C}\to(0,\infty)$ are Lipschitz on $\mathcal{C}$. Viewing the diffusion matrix as $\sigma(x)I_d$, there exist constants $M>0$ and $\alpha\geq0$ such that for all $x,y\in\mathcal{C}$,
\begin{equation}
\|b(x)-b(y)\| \leq M\|x-y\|, \quad \|\sigma(x)I_d-\sigma(y)I_d\|_{\mathrm{F}} \leq \sqrt{\alpha}\|x-y\|,
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-4 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma:bound:bm}
There exists a universal constant $C>0$ such that, for every $T\geq e$ and $0<\eta\leq1$,
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s,t\in[0,T],|s-t|\leq \eta}\|W_t- W_s\|^2\right]
\leq Cd\eta\log(T/\eta).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:lemma:bound:bm}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma:bound:K}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:smooth} and~\ref{asmpt:set}, suppose that the initial law is supported in $\mathcal C_{r_0}:=\{x\in\mathcal C:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0\}$ for some $r_0>0$. Then, for every $t>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hat:K:L2:bound}
\mathbb E\left[|\hat K|_t^2\right]\leq At^2+Bt,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
A  :=3r_0^{-2}\left(4R^2B_b^2+S_\sigma^4\right),
\qquad
B  :=24r_0^{-2}R^2S_\sigma^2.
\end{align}
For every $T=k\eta$ with $k\in\mathbb N$ and $0<\eta\leq1$, the bound satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bar:K:L2:bound}
\mathbb E\left[|\bar K|_T^2\right]\leq \bar A T^2+\bar B T,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
\bar A
:=3r_0^{-2}\left(4R^2B_b^2+2B_b^4+8S_\sigma^4\right),
\qquad
\bar B
:=3r_0^{-2}\left(8R^2S_\sigma^2+16S_\sigma^4\right),
\end{align}
with constants $B_b$ and $S_{\sigma}$ defined in~\eqref{eqn:b:sigma:bdd}.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:lemma:bound:K}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma:alg2bri}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:smooth} and~\ref{asmpt:set}, suppose that the initial law is supported in $\mathcal C_{r_0}:=\{x\in\mathcal C:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0\}$ for some $r_0>0$. Let $T=k\eta\geq e$ and $0<\eta\leq1$. Set
\begin{align}
H_K
&:=\sqrt A+\sqrt{B/e}+\sqrt{\bar A}+\sqrt{\bar B/e},\label{eq:bridge:HK}\\
C_0
&:=2B_b^2+4CdS_\sigma^2
+4H_K\left(\sqrt2B_b+2\sqrt{Cd}\,S_\sigma\right),\label{eq:bridge:C0}
\end{align}
where $C$ is the universal constant in Lemma~\ref{lemma:bound:bm}, and $A,B,\bar A,\bar B$ are defined in Lemma~\ref{lemma:bound:K}. Then,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bridge:uniform}
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}
\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
\leq C_0\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:lemma:alg2bri}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{global:error:2}
Under Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:strongly:convex},~\ref{asmpt:smooth} and \ref{asmpt:set}, moreover, we assume that the common initial law is supported in $\mathcal C_{r_0}:=\{x\in\mathcal C:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0\}$ for some $r_0>0$. For any $k \in \mathbb{N}$, $0<\eta\leq1$, and $T = k\eta \geq e$, we have
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{W}^2_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar{X}_T),\mathrm{Law}(X_T)\right)  \leq \sqrt{\eta}T\log(T/\eta)
\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{M^2+4\alpha}{2m-1-4\alpha}}\right)^2 C_0,
\end{equation}
provided $2m>1+4\alpha$, where $C_0$ is given in \eqref{eq:bridge:C0}.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:global:error:2}.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:final}
Suppose that Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:strongly:convex},~\ref{asmpt:smooth}, and~\ref{asmpt:set} hold, and that the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square} hold. Assume that the common initial law is supported in $\mathcal C_{r_0}:=\{x\in\mathcal C:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0\}$ for some $r_0>0$, and
$$
2m>1+4\alpha.
$$
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thm:final:Cbar}
\bar C_0:=\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{M^2+4\alpha}{2m-1-4\alpha}}\right)\sqrt{C_0},
\end{equation}
where $C_0$ is given in Theorem~\ref{global:error:2}. Then, for every $k\in\mathbb N$ and $0<\eta\leq1$ such that $k\eta\geq e$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thm:final:explicit}
\mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(x_k),\pi)
\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha_0k\eta/(2C_P)}
+\bar C_0\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2},
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_0$ and $C_P$ are defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}. In the special case $\sigma\equiv1$, one has $U=U_0$ and RALMC reduces to PLMC for the differentiable target potential. If $\sigma\equiv1$ and $B_b,M,m,R,r_0$ are independent of $d$, then $\bar C_0=\mathcal O(d)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thm:final:dimension}
\mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(x_k),\pi)
\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha_0k\eta/(2C_P)}
+\widetilde{\mathcal O}\left(d\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:thm:final}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:complexity}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:final}, fix $\varepsilon>0$ and set
$$
    A_0:=2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4},
    \qquad
    T_\varepsilon:=\max\left\{e,
    \frac{2C_P}{\alpha_0}\log\left(1 \vee \frac{2A_0}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}.
$$
Choose an integer $k$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:complexity:valid:k}
    k\geq\max\{3,T_\varepsilon\},
    \qquad
    \frac{k}{(\log k)^2}
    \geq
    \frac{16\bar C_0^4T_\varepsilon^3}{\varepsilon^4},
\end{equation}
and set $\eta:=T_\varepsilon/k$. Then $0<\eta\leq1$, $k\eta=T_\varepsilon\geq e$, and
$$
    \mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(x_k),\pi)\leq\varepsilon.
$$
In particular,
$$
    k
    =\widetilde{\mathcal O}\left(
    T_\varepsilon+\frac{\bar C_0^4T_\varepsilon^3}{\varepsilon^4}
    \right).
$$
Under the additional scaling assumptions in the second part of Theorem~\ref{thm:final}, and when $A_0,C_P,\alpha_0$ are dimension independent, this becomes
\begin{align}\label{under:additional}
    \eta=\widetilde{\mathcal O}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^4}{d^4}\right),
    \qquad
    k=\widetilde{\mathcal O}\left(\frac{d^4}{\varepsilon^4}\right).
\end{align}
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:cor:complexity}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- assumption-5: body_md -->
\label{assump:elliptic}
There exist constants $0<\lambda\leq\Lambda<\infty$ such that, for every $x\in\overline{\mathcal C}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:uniform:elliptic}
    \lambda I_d
    \preceq
    [\sigma(x)I_d][\sigma(x)I_d]^\top
    =a(x)I_d
    \preceq
    \Lambda I_d.
\end{equation}
<!-- end assumption-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:const:strong:euler}
Consider $U_0\equiv c_0$. Suppose Assumption~\ref{asmpt:set} holds and $\sigma$ is Lipschitz on $\mathcal C$. Write
$$
\left\|\left[\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)\right]I_d\right\|_{\mathrm F}\leq\ell_\sigma\|x-y\|.
$$
For every fixed $s>0$, there is a finite constant $I_s$, given explicitly in \eqref{eq:const:Is}, such that, for every integer $n\geq1$ and $\eta=s/n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:strong:euler:log-rate}
\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}
\left(\mathbb E_x\left\|\bar X_s-X_s\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\leq I_s\left[\eta\left(1+\log\frac{s}{\eta}\right)\right]^{1/4}.
\end{equation}
The two processes use the same Brownian motion and initial point. The bound is uniform over all initial points in $\mathcal C$, including its boundary.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:prop:const:strong:euler}.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:const:semigroup:stability}
Suppose $U_0\equiv c_0$, Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:set}, \ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity}, and \ref{assump:elliptic} hold, and $\sigma$ is Lipschitz with constant $\ell_\sigma$ as above. Let $C_P$ be the Poincar\'e constant in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}, and set $g_0=\lambda/C_P$. Write $\kappa_t(x,y)=dP_t(x,\cdot)/d\pi(y)$ for the heat kernel relative to $\pi$. Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(iii) ensures that the following extrema are finite and strictly positive. For a fixed $s>0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:heatkernel:constants}
m_s:=\min_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_s(x,y)>0,\quad
M_s:=\max_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_s(x,y)<\infty,\quad
H_s:=e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\sqrt{\frac{M_s}{2e\,g_0s\,m_s}}.
\end{equation}
Then, for any probability measures $\mu,\nu$ on $\mathcal C$,
\begin{align}
\mathcal W_2(\mu P_t,\nu P_t)&\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2t}\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu), \qquad 0\leq t< 2s,\label{eq:const:short:stability}\\
\mathcal W_2(\mu P_t,\nu P_t)&\leq H_s e^{-g_0(t-2s)}\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu), \qquad t\geq2s.\label{eq:const:long:stability}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:const:semigroup:stability}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:U:0:const:strong}
Under the assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:const:semigroup:stability}, fix $s>0$ independently of the total running time and of the desired accuracy. Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:Fs}
B_s:=1+e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}+\frac{H_s}{1-e^{-g_0s}},
\qquad F_s:=B_sI_s.
\end{equation}
For any initial law $\mu_0$ on $\mathcal C$, integers $m,n\geq1$, and $\eta=s/n$, $T=ms$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:uniform:comparison}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_T),\mu_0P_T\right)
\leq F_s\left[\eta \left(1+\log\frac{s}{\eta}\right)\right]^{1/4}.
\end{equation}
If also $\mu_0\ll\pi$ and $\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)<\infty$, denote $A_0:=2^{3/4}R(\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi))^{1/4}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const-ref-unconditional-W2}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_T),\pi\right)
\leq A_0e^{-g_0T/2}
+F_s\left[\eta\left(1+\log\frac{s}{\eta}\right)\right]^{1/4}.
\end{equation}
The constant $F_s$ is independent of $m$, $T$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:thm:U:0:const:strong}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:complexity:const}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:U:0:const:strong}, including its initial $\chi^2$ condition, fix $s>0$. Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose integers $m,n\geq1$ satisfying
$$
m\geq\frac{2}{g_0s}\log\left(1+\frac{2A_0}{\varepsilon}\right),
\qquad
\frac{n}{(1 + \log n)}\geq\frac{16F_s^4s}{\varepsilon^4}.
$$
Then $\eta=s/n$ and $k=mn$ give $\mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{k\eta}),\pi)\leq\varepsilon$. For fixed model parameters and fixed $s$, one can choose $k=\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\varepsilon^{-4})$ as $\varepsilon\rightarrow0$. This order concerns accuracy only, it does not assert polynomial dependence on dimension or all model parameters.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:cor:complexity:const}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:const:conditioning}
Under the assumptions of Lemma~\ref{lem:const:semigroup:stability}, define
\begin{align*}
A_{\rm hk}&:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}\left\{\kappa_{2/g_0}(x,x)-1\right\}
=\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}\chi^2\left(P_{1/g_0}(x,\cdot)\Vert\pi\right)<\infty,\\
a_*&:=2+\log\max\left\{1,2A_{\rm hk}\right\},\qquad s_*:=a_*/g_0.
\end{align*}
Then $m_{s_*}\geq1/2$, $M_{s_*}\leq3/2$, and
$$
H_{s_*}\leq \exp\!\left(\frac{\ell_\sigma^2}{g_0}a_*\right)\sqrt{\frac{3}{2a_*}}.
$$
If, additionally, $\ell_\sigma^2/g_0\leq\ell_*$ for a specified constant $\ell_*\geq0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:conditioning:Fs}
F_{s_*}\leq6\exp(6\ell_*)\max\left\{1,2A_{\rm hk}\right\}^{3\ell_*}\sqrt{G_{s_*}},
\end{equation}
where $G_s$, defined in \eqref{eq:const:Is}, has only dependence on $s$.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:const:conditioning}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- assumption-6: body_md -->
\label{assumption:f:Gaussian:smoothing}
The function $f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}$ is continuously differentiable. There exist $L_f,m_f>0$ such that, for all $x,y\in\mathcal C$,
\begin{align}
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|&\leq L_f\|x-y\|,\qquad
\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle
\geq m_f\|x-y\|^2.
\end{align}
<!-- end assumption-6 -->
<!-- assumption-7: body_md -->
\label{assumption:g:Gaussian:smoothing}
The function $g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}$ is Lipschitz with constant $K$ and weakly convex with parameter $\rho$: for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}$ and $\lambda\in[0,1]$,
\begin{align}
|g(x)-g(y)|&\leq K\|x-y\|,\\
g(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y)
&\leq \lambda g(x)+(1-\lambda)g(y)
+\frac{\rho}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)\|x-y\|^2.
\label{assumption:g:convex:Gaussian:smoothing}
\end{align}
<!-- end assumption-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma:propositions of Gaussian smoothing}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:set}, \ref{assumption:f:Gaussian:smoothing}, and \ref{assumption:g:Gaussian:smoothing} hold. Set $h_{\mu}:=U-U_{\mu}=g-g_{\mu}$. Then
\begin{gather}
\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}|h_{\mu}(x)|
\leq K\mu\sqrt d, \label{eq:Gaussian:hmu:sup}\\
\sup_{\substack{x,y\in\mathcal C\\x\neq y}}
\frac{|h_{\mu}(x)-h_{\mu}(y)|}{\|x-y\|}
\leq 2K, \label{eq:Gaussian:hmu:Lipschitz}\\
\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}\|\nabla U_{\mu}(x)\|
\leq \|\nabla f(0)\|+L_fR+K. \label{eq:Gaussian:Gmu:explicit}
\end{gather}
$g_{\mu}$ is weakly convex with parameter $\rho$ and has Lipschitz gradient with constant $K\sqrt d/\mu$. If $m_f>\rho$, then $U_{\mu}$ is strongly convex with constant $m_f-\rho$ on $\mathcal C$ and has
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Lip}(\nabla U_\mu)\leq L_f+\frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:Gaussian:preliminary:bounds}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemma:Gaussian:sufficient:conditions}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:set}, \ref{assumption:f:Gaussian:smoothing}, and \ref{assumption:g:Gaussian:smoothing} hold with $m_f>\rho$. If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gaussian:perturbation:small}
    \varepsilon_{b,\mu}
    +2d\varepsilon_{\sigma,\mu}^2
    <m_f-\rho-\frac12,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gaussian:exact:coefficients}
    b(x):=-\nabla U_\mu(x)e^{h_\mu(x)},
    \qquad
    \sigma(x):=e^{h_\mu(x)/2}
\end{equation}
satisfy Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:strongly:convex} and \ref{asmpt:smooth} with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gaussian:m:M:alpha}
    m_\mu
    :=m_f-\rho-\varepsilon_{b,\mu},\qquad
    M_\mu
    :=L_f+\frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}+\varepsilon_{b,\mu},\qquad
    \alpha_\mu
    :=d\varepsilon_{\sigma,\mu}^2.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
$$
    2m_\mu>1+4\alpha_\mu,
$$
and hence the coefficient assumptions of Theorem~\ref{global:error:2} hold.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:Gaussian:sufficient:conditions}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma:Gaussian:MC:error}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:set}, \ref{assumption:f:Gaussian:smoothing}, and \ref{assumption:g:Gaussian:smoothing} hold, condition~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:perturbation:small} is satisfied, and $m_\mu,M_\mu,\alpha_\mu$ are defined by \eqref{eq:Gaussian:m:M:alpha}. Lemma~\ref{lemma:Gaussian:sufficient:conditions} then gives $2m_\mu>1+4\alpha_\mu$. Define
\begin{align}
&A_{\mu,N}
:=\frac{K^2d(d+2)}{N}e^{2K\mu\sqrt d+2K^2\mu^2/N}
+\left(\|\nabla f(0)\|+L_fR+K\right)^2e^{2K\mu\sqrt d}
\left(e^{2K^2\mu^2/N}-1\right),
\label{eq:Gaussian:A}\\
&B_{\mu,N}
:=e^{K\mu\sqrt d}
\left(e^{K^2\mu^2/(2N)}-1\right).
\label{eq:Gaussian:B}
\end{align}
Then, for every $k\in\mathbb N$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gaussian:eta:conservative}
0<\eta\leq
\min\left\{1,
\frac{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}
{2\bigl(\max\{0,M_\mu^2-2m_\mu\}+M_\mu^2\bigr)}
\right\},
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{align}
\mathcal W_2^2(\nu_k,\tilde\nu_k)
&\leq
\frac{2}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}
\left((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\right).
\label{eq:Gaussian:MC:W2}
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:Gaussian:MC:error}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{theorem:Gaussian:MC:convergence}
Suppose Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:set}, \ref{assumption:f:Gaussian:smoothing}, and \ref{assumption:g:Gaussian:smoothing} hold. Fix $\mu\in(0,1]$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Gaussian:perturbation:small}, and suppose Assumption~\ref{asmpt:continuous:regularity} holds with $U_0=U_\mu$. Suppose also that $\pi$ satisfies the Poincar\'{e} inequality used in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}, that $\nu_0=\mu_0\ll\pi$ with $\chi^2(\nu_0\Vert\pi)<\infty$, and that the common initial law is supported in $\mathcal C_{r_0}:=\{x\in\mathcal C:\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0\}$ for some $r_0>0$. Then, for every $N\geq1$, $k\in\mathbb N$ with $k\eta\geq e$, and every $\eta$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Gaussian:eta:conservative}, the law of RALMC iterates in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:MC:RALMC} satisfies
\begin{align}
\mathcal W_2(\tilde\nu_k,\pi)
&\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\nu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}
e^{-\alpha_{0,\mu}k\eta/(2C_P)}
+\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2}\bar C_{0,\mu}\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left(\frac{2}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}
\left((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\right)\right)^{1/2}.
\label{eq:Gaussian:final:W2}
\end{align}
Here $C_P$ is defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}, $A_{\mu,N}$ and $B_{\mu,N}$ are defined in \eqref{eq:Gaussian:A}-\eqref{eq:Gaussian:B}. Consequently, for every fixed $\mu$ satisfying these assumptions and every $\varepsilon>0$, there exist $T=k\eta$, $\eta>0$, and $N\geq1$ such that $\mathcal W_2(\tilde\nu_k,\pi)\leq\varepsilon$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:Gaussian:MC:convergence}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:GS:sharp:coefficients}
Suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility}
\kappa>2K(\kappa R+K)+e^{2K\mu}K^2.
\end{equation}
Then, for every $\delta>0$ and $x,y\in\mathcal C$,
\begin{align}
(b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y))(x-y)
&\leq-e^{-K\mu}\bigl[\kappa-2K(\kappa R+K)\bigr]|x-y|^2,\nonumber\\
|b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|&\leq e^{K\mu}\bigl[\kappa+K/\mu+2K(\kappa R+K)\bigr]|x-y|,\nonumber\\
|\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|^2
&\leq e^{K\mu}K^2|x-y|^2.
\label{eq:GS:sharp:bounds}
\end{align}
All three bounds are independent of $\delta$. In contrast, $U_\delta''(0)=\kappa+K/\delta$. For any fixed $\kappa,R,\mu>0$, condition \eqref{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility} holds for all sufficiently small $K>0$, without restricting $\delta$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:GS:sharp:coefficients}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:GS:sharp:contraction}
Assume~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility} and~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:eta}, with $q_\eta$ defined above. Let $\nu$ and $\nu'$ be any two initial probability distributions on $\mathcal C$. For a transition kernel $Q$, write $(\nu Q)(A):=\int_{\mathcal C}Q(x,A)\,\nu(dx)$ for every Borel set $A\subseteq\mathcal C$, this is the distribution after one update from an initial state with law $\nu$. Then, for every $\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:contraction}
\mathcal W_2\left(\nu Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta},\nu'Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}\right)
\leq q_\eta\mathcal W_2(\nu,\nu'),\qquad 0<q_\eta<1.
\end{equation}
The kernel has a unique invariant probability measure $\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$. Starting from $\nu_0:=\nu$, let $\nu_k:=\nu_0(Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta})^k$ denote the law after $k$ RALMC updates, where the power denotes $k$ successive applications of the transition kernel. Taking $\nu'=\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$ in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:contraction} and iterating gives, for every integer $k\geq0$,
$$
\mathcal W_2\left(\nu_k,\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}\right)
\leq q_\eta^k\mathcal W_2(\nu_0,\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}).
$$
Both the admissible stepsize interval and the contraction factor are independent of $\delta$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:GS:sharp:contraction}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:GS:sharp:separation}
Assume \eqref{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility}, and let $\eta_c$ be defined by~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:eta}. Given $\delta>0$, choose $\eta$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:separation:condition}
0<\eta<\eta_c,\qquad
\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)>1+p_\eta^{-1},
\qquad
p_\eta:=\mathbb P\left(\left|\sqrt{2\eta}G\right|<R\right).
\end{equation}
With this same $\eta$ in both kernels in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:kernels}, normalized RALMC satisfies the ratio bound~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:RALMC:pointwise}, in particular with $y=0$, whereas the actual projected PLMC kernel satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:PL:expansion}
\liminf_{\substack{x\to0\\x\neq0}}
\frac{\mathcal W_2(Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(x,\cdot),
Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(0,\cdot))}{|x|}
\geq [\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)-1]p_\eta>1.
\end{equation}
The comparison range is nonempty for sufficiently small $\delta$: every fixed $\eta\in(0,\eta_c)$ satisfies \eqref{eq:GS:sharp:separation:condition} whenever
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:delta:range}
0<\delta<\frac{\eta K}{1+p_\eta^{-1}}.
\end{equation}
Moreover, fix any finite $\eta_{\max}>0$. If $0<\eta\leq\eta_{\max}$ and PLMC is globally nonexpansive in $\mathcal W_2$, then necessarily
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:PL:necessary}
\eta\leq\frac{1+p_\eta^{-1}}{\kappa+K/\delta}
\leq\frac{1+p_{\eta_{\max}}^{-1}}{\kappa+K/\delta}.
\end{equation}
Thus PLMC nonexpansiveness in any fixed bounded stepsize range requires $\eta=O(\delta)$ as $\delta\rightarrow0$, whereas RALMC admits a $\delta$-independent contraction interval under the same stationary noise normalization.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:proof:GS:sharp:separation}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:dirichlet:comparison}
Suppose that $U$ is continuous on $\overline{\mathcal C}$ and that $a_{\rm n}$ is measurable with the positive finite bounds stated below. On the common form domain $H^1(\mathcal C)\subset L^2(\pi)$, define the Neumann Dirichlet forms of PLD and normalized RALD by
$$
    \mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f):=\int_{\mathcal C}\|\nabla f\|^2d\pi,
    \qquad
    \mathcal E_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}(f)
    :=\int_{\mathcal C}a_{\rm n}\|\nabla f\|^2d\pi.
$$
The bounded positive target density and scaling factor make both form norms equivalent to the usual $H^1(\mathcal C)$ norm. These are therefore closed forms with a common domain, their spectral gaps are defined by
$$
    \lambda_{\rm PLD}
    :=
    \inf_{f\in H^1(\mathcal C):\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)>0}
    \frac{\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)},
    \qquad
    \lambda_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}
    :=
    \inf_{f\in H^1(\mathcal C):\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)>0}
    \frac{\mathcal E_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}(f)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)}.
$$
If $ a_{{\rm n},\min}:=\inf_{x\in\mathcal C}a_{\rm n}(x)>0,
    \,
    a_{{\rm n},\max}:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}a_{\rm n}(x)<\infty, $ then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gap:comparison}
    a_{{\rm n},\min}\lambda_{\rm PLD}
    \leq
    \lambda_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}
    \leq
    a_{{\rm n},\max}\lambda_{\rm PLD}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{proof:prop:dirichlet:comparison}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:bottleneck:minorization}
Fix $x\in B$ with $a_{\rm n}(x)>1$ and $r_x:=\operatorname{dist}(x,D_+)>0$. Then, for every $\eta>0$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:bottleneck:pointwise:ratio}
& \frac{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)}{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)}
\nonumber
\\
& \, \geq a_{\rm n}(x)^{-d/2}\exp\Bigg\{
\frac{r_x^2}{4\eta}\left(1-\frac1{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right)-\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)}2\|\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\|
-\frac\eta4 a_{\rm n}(x)\|\nabla U_0(x)\|^2\Bigg\}.
\end{align}
For a uniform comparison on $B$, define
$$
\rho:=\inf_{x\in B}a_{\rm n}(x),\qquad
A:=\sup_{x\in B}a_{\rm n}(x),\qquad
r:=\operatorname{dist}(B,D_+).
$$
Suppose $1<\rho\leq A<\infty$, $r>0$, gradients are bounded on $B$. Then, for every $x\in B$ and $\eta>0$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:bottleneck:finite:ratio}
& \frac{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)}{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)}
\nonumber
\\
& \, \geq A^{-d/2}\exp\Bigg\{
\frac{r^2}{4\eta}\left(1-\frac1\rho\right)-\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)}2
\sup_{z\in B}\|\nabla U(z)-\nabla U_0(z)\|
-\frac\eta4\sup_{z\in B}a_{\rm n}(z)\|\nabla U_0(z)\|^2\Bigg\}.
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:prop:bottleneck:minorization}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:bottleneck:minorization}
Suppose $D_+$ is a nonempty open subset of $\operatorname{int}(\mathcal C)$. Fix $x\in B$ and assume $r_x=\operatorname{dist}(x,D_+)>0$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bottleneck:exact:rates}
    \lim_{\eta\rightarrow0}
    \eta\log\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)
    =-\frac{r_x^2}{4a_{\rm n}(x)},\quad
    \lim_{\eta\rightarrow0}
    \eta\log\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)
    =-\frac{r_x^2}{4}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bottleneck:probability:ratio}
    \lim_{\eta\rightarrow0}
    \eta\log
    \frac{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)}
         {\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)}
    =\frac{r_x^2}{4}
      \left(1-\frac{1}{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right).
\end{equation}
In particular, $a_{\rm n}(x)>1$ is both necessary and sufficient for a strictly positive exponential rate in this same event comparison. Under the uniform assumptions of Proposition~\ref{prop:bottleneck:minorization}, the right hand side of~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:probability:ratio} is at least
$$
    \frac{r^2}{4}\left(1-\frac1\rho\right)>0,
    \qquad x\in B.
$$
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{sec:cor:bottleneck:minorization}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
\emph{(i)} For a Neumann test function $f$, Lemma~\ref{lem:reflected:timechange} gives
$$
\frac{P_tf(x)-f(x)}{t}=\frac1t\mathbb E^x\left[\int_0^t\mathcal Lf(X_s)\,ds\right]\rightarrow\mathcal Lf(x).
$$
The limit follows from continuity of the paths and bounded continuity of $\mathcal Lf$, proving~\eqref{eqn:generator}.

\emph{(ii)} Set $w=Z^{-1}e^{-U}$ and $q=Z^{-1}e^{-U_0}$. Continuity and compactness give constants $0<c_-\leq c_+<\infty$ bounding both weights. Hence,
$$
\begin{aligned}
c_-\|f\|_{H^1(\mathcal C)}^2 &\leq\|f\|_{L^2(\pi)}^2+\mathcal E(f,f)=\int_{\mathcal C}\left(|f|^2w+\|\nabla f\|^2q\right)\,dx
\leq c_+\|f\|_{H^1(\mathcal C)}^2.
\end{aligned}
$$
Completeness and density of $H^1(\mathcal C)$ therefore make $\mathcal E$ closed and densely defined on $L^2(\pi)$.  
For the truncation $\Phi(r)=\min\{1,\max\{0,r\}\}$, the Sobolev chain rule gives $|\nabla\Phi(f)|\leq|\nabla f|$ almost everywhere, so $\mathcal E(\Phi(f),\Phi(f))\leq\mathcal E(f,f)$, which is the Markov property. Smooth restrictions are dense in $H^1(\mathcal C)$ by the extension property of the domain, and dense in $C(\mathcal C)$ in the uniform norm, giving regularity. If $f$ is constant on a neighborhood of the support of $g$, the gradient integral defining $\mathcal E(f,g)$ vanishes, giving strong locality.

Let $\mathsf A\geq0$ be the associated self adjoint operator and $S_t=e^{-t\mathsf A}$. For a Neumann test function $f$ and $g\in H^1(\mathcal C)$, integration by parts gives
$$
\mathcal E(f,g)=-\frac1Z\int_{\mathcal C}g\,\nabla\cdot\left(e^{-U_0}\nabla f\right)\,dx
=-\langle\mathcal Lf,g\rangle_{L^2(\pi)}.
$$
Thus $-\mathsf Af=\mathcal Lf$ and $\mathsf A1=0$. The associated conservative diffusion solves the Neumann martingale problem for $\pi$ almost every initial point by \cite[Theorems~7.2.1, 7.2.2 and~5.2.2, Eq.~(5.2.26)]{FOT2011}, using boundedness and a countable dense family of Neumann tests in the norm $\|f\|_\infty+\|\mathcal Lf\|_\infty$. Uniqueness in Lemma~\ref{lem:reflected:timechange} identifies $S_t=P_t$ on $L^2(\pi)$. Hence $P_t1=1$, and spectral calculus gives $P_tf\in\mathcal D(\mathsf A)$ for $t>0$ and
$$
\frac{d}{dt}\|P_tf\|_{L^2(\pi)}^2
=-2\langle P_tf,\mathsf AP_tf\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} =-2\mathcal E(P_tf,P_tf).
$$

\emph{(iii)} On the closed convex state space $\mathcal C$, the unweighted Neumann form has the complete Euclidean intrinsic metric. Convexity gives volume doubling and the Poincar\'e inequality on $D_r=\mathcal C\cap B(x,r)$,
$$
\begin{gathered}
|D_{2r}|\leq2^d|D_r|,\quad f_{D_r}:=\frac1{|D_r|}\int_{D_r}f\,dx, \quad \int_{D_r}|f-f_{D_r}|^2dx\leq c_dr^2\int_{D_r}\|\nabla f\|^2dx.
\end{gathered}
$$
The bounds on $w,q$ make the weighted measure and form uniformly comparable to the unweighted ones, and their intrinsic metrics equivalent. Thus \cite[Theorem~3.5 and Corollary~3.6]{sturm1996localIII} gives the parabolic Harnack inequality, and \cite[Proposition~3.1, Theorems~4.1 and~4.8]{sturm1996localIII} gives a jointly continuous, strictly positive kernel $\kappa_t(x,y)$ on $(0,\infty)\times\mathcal C^2$, including the boundary. 

To justify continuity in the initial point, couple the base diffusions $Y^{x_n}$ and $Y^x$ with the same Brownian motion, where $x_n\to x$. Additive noise cancels in their difference, and the convex reflection sign gives
$$
\sup_{0\leq u\leq T}\left\|Y_u^{x_n}-Y_u^x\right\|\leq e^{\operatorname{Lip}(\nabla U_0)T}\|x_n-x\|.
$$
Since $a^{-1}$ is uniformly continuous on $\mathcal C$, the clocks $A_u^{x_n}=\int_0^u a(Y_r^{x_n})^{-1}dr$ converge uniformly to $A_u^x$ on bounded intervals. Their slopes lie between $1/a^*$ and $1/a_*$, so their inverse clocks also converge uniformly on bounded intervals. Continuity of the base paths now gives $X_t^{x_n}=Y^{x_n}_{\tau_t^{x_n}}\to Y^x_{\tau_t^x}=X_t^x$ almost surely. For bounded continuous $f$, dominated convergence yields $P_tf(x_n)\to P_tf(x)$, proving the Feller property.
For $f\in C(\mathcal C)$, the identity $P_tf(x)=\int_{\mathcal C}\kappa_t(x,y)f(y)\pi(dy)$ from part~(ii) therefore extends from $\pi$ almost every $x$ to every $x$ by continuity and full support of $\pi$.  
The Lebesgue density is $p_t(x,y)=\kappa_t(x,y)w(y)$. For fixed $t>0$, both factors are continuous and strictly positive on the compact set $\mathcal C^2$. Consequently,
$$
0<\min_{x,y\in\mathcal C}p_t(x,y)\leq\max_{x,y\in\mathcal C}p_t(x,y)<\infty,
$$
which proves the asserted extrema.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
By Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(ii), $P_t$ is self adjoint on $L^2(\pi)$ and satisfies $P_t1=1$. Hence, for every bounded measurable $f$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
    \int_{\mathcal C}P_tf\,d\pi
    &=\langle1,P_tf\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} =\langle P_t1,f\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} =\int_{\mathcal C}f\,d\pi.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $\pi$ is invariant.  
To prove uniqueness, fix $t>0$. Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(iii) gives a jointly continuous, strictly positive transition density on $\mathcal C^2$. Since $\pi(dy)=w(y)dy$ with $w$ continuous and strictly positive, the density $\kappa_t(x,y)=dP_t(x,\cdot)/d\pi(y)$ has the same properties. Compactness therefore gives $\min_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_t(x,y)>0$. Set $\varepsilon_t=\frac12\min_{x,y\in\mathcal C}\kappa_t(x,y)$. Since $\int_{\mathcal C}\kappa_t(x,y)\pi(dy)=1$, we have $0<\varepsilon_t\leq1/2$. Thus
$$
P_t(x,dy)=\varepsilon_t\pi(dy)+(1-\varepsilon_t)\frac{\kappa_t(x,y)-\varepsilon_t}{1-\varepsilon_t}\pi(dy).
$$
The second fraction defines a Markov kernel, which contracts total variation. If $\nu$ is another invariant probability measure, then
$$
\mathrm{TV}(\nu,\pi)=\mathrm{TV}(\nu P_t,\pi P_t)\leq(1-\varepsilon_t)\mathrm{TV}(\nu,\pi).
$$
Since $\varepsilon_t>0$, this forces $\mathrm{TV}(\nu,\pi)=0$, proving uniqueness.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
First, a finite Poincar\'e constant exists under the stated assumptions. The density $w=Z^{-1}e^{-U}$ has bounds $0<w_{\min}\leq w\leq w_{\max}<\infty$. Let $C_{\mathcal C}$ be a Lebesgue Poincar\'e constant on the bounded convex domain and $f_{\mathcal C}=|\mathcal C|^{-1}\int_{\mathcal C}f\,dx$. For $f\in H^1(\mathcal C)$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)
&\leq w_{\max}\int_{\mathcal C}|f-f_{\mathcal C}|^2dx\leq w_{\max}C_{\mathcal C}\int_{\mathcal C}\|\nabla f\|^2dx
\leq\frac{w_{\max}}{w_{\min}}C_{\mathcal C}\int_{\mathcal C}\|\nabla f\|^2d\pi.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $C_P=(w_{\max}/w_{\min})C_{\mathcal C}$ is admissible. The convergence argument below uses any valid $C_P$, not necessarily this bound. Denote $h_0:=d\mu_0/d\pi$. The assumption $\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi)<\infty$ gives $h_0\in L^2(\pi)$. Since the semigroup is self adjoint by Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(ii), the density evolves according to
$$
    h_t=P_t^*h_0=P_th_0.
$$
For every $t>0$, Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(ii) gives $h_t\in\mathcal D(\mathcal L)$. Since $P_t1=1$ and $\int h_0d\pi=1$, one has $\int h_td\pi=1$. Therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
    \frac{d}{dt}\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)
    &=\frac{d}{dt}\|h_t-1\|_{L^2(\pi)}^2
    =2\langle h_t-1,\mathcal Lh_t\rangle_{L^2(\pi)}\\
    &=2\langle h_t,\mathcal Lh_t\rangle_{L^2(\pi)} =-2\mathcal E(h_t,h_t) =-2\int_{\mathcal C}e^{U-U_0}\|\nabla h_t\|^2d\pi.
\end{aligned}
$$
By the definition of $\alpha_0$ and the Poincar\'e inequality,
$$
\begin{aligned}
    \mathcal E(h_t,h_t)
    &\geq\alpha_0\int_{\mathcal C}\|\nabla h_t\|^2d\pi
    \geq\frac{\alpha_0}{C_P}\operatorname{Var}_\pi(h_t)\\
    &=\frac{\alpha_0}{C_P}\|h_t-1\|_{L^2(\pi)}^2 =\frac{\alpha_0}{C_P}\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi).
\end{aligned}
$$
Hence, for every $t>0$,
$$
    \frac{d}{dt}\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)
    \leq-\frac{2\alpha_0}{C_P}\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi).
$$
Applying Gr\"{o}nwall's inequality on $[s,t]$ with $0<s<t$ gives
$$
    \chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)
    \leq e^{-2\alpha_0(t-s)/C_P}\chi^2(\mu_s\Vert\pi).
$$
Strong continuity of $P_s$ on $L^2(\pi)$ yields $h_s\to h_0$ in $L^2(\pi)$ as $s\to0$. Letting $s\to0$ proves
$$
    \chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)
    \leq e^{-2\alpha_0t/C_P}\chi^2(\mu_0\Vert\pi).
$$
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Writing $h_t=d\mu_t/d\pi$ and applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives
$$
    \mathrm{TV}(\mu_t,\pi)
    =\frac12\int_{\mathcal C}|h_t-1|\,d\pi
    \leq\frac12\sqrt{\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)}.
$$
Choose a maximal coupling $(Y,Z)$ of $\mu_t$ and $\pi$, so that $\mathbb P(Y\neq Z)=\mathrm{TV}(\mu_t,\pi)$. Since $Y,Z\in\mathcal C\subset B(0,R)$,
$$
\mathcal W_2^2(\mu_t,\pi)\leq\mathbb E\|Y-Z\|^2\leq4R^2\mathbb P(Y\neq Z)=4R^2\mathrm{TV}(\mu_t,\pi).
$$
Consequently,
$$
    \mathcal W_2(\mu_t,\pi)
    \leq\sqrt2R\,\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)^{1/4}
    \leq2^{3/4}R\,\chi^2(\mu_t\Vert\pi)^{1/4}.
$$
Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square} proves the desired bound.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
We adapt the proof of Lemma 2.3 in~\cite{bubeck2018sampling}. Let $N:=\lceil T/\eta\rceil$. For $i=0,\dots,N-1$, define
$$
Y_i:=\sup_{u\in[i\eta,(i+2)\eta]}
\left\|W_u-W_{(i+1)\eta}\right\|.
$$

We first reduce the modulus of continuity to $\max_{0\leq i\leq N-1}Y_i$. Fix $s,t\in[0,T]$ with $|t-s|\leq\eta$. By symmetry, assume $s\leq t$. If $s=T$, then $s=t$. Otherwise, let $i=\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\leq N-1$. Then $s,t\in[i\eta,(i+2)\eta]$ and by the triangle inequality,
$$
\|W_t-W_s\|
\leq \left\|W_t-W_{(i+1)\eta}\right\|+\left\|W_s-W_{(i+1)\eta}\right\|
\leq 2Y_i
\leq 2\max_{0\leq j\leq N-1}Y_j.
$$
Taking the supremum over $|t-s|\leq \eta$ yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:reduce_to_Y}
    \sup_{s,t\in[0,T],|t-s|\leq \eta}\|W_t-W_s\|^2 \leq 4\max_{0\leq i\leq N-1}Y_i^2.
\end{equation}
Next, we bound $\mathbb E\left[ \max_{0\leq i\leq N-1}Y_i^2 \right]$ via an $L^p$ maximal inequality. For any $p\geq 1$, by Jensen's inequality,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:max_lp}
    \mathbb E\left[ \max_{0\leq i\leq N-1}Y_i^2 \right]
    \leq \mathbb E\left[ \left( \sum_{i=0}^{N-1}Y_i^{2p} \right)^{1/p} \right]
    \leq \left( \mathbb E\left[ \sum_{i=0}^{N-1}Y_i^{2p} \right] \right)^{1/p}
    \leq N^{1/p}\|Y_0^2\|_p
    =N^{1/p}\|Y_0\|_{2p}^2,
\end{equation}
where we used the fact that $(Y_i)_{i\geq 0}$ are identically distributed by stationary increments. We now bound $\|Y_0\|_{2p}$. Note that
$$
Y_0=\sup_{u\in[0,2\eta]}\|W_u-W_{\eta}\|
\leq \sup_{v\in[0,\eta]}\|W_{\eta+v}-W_{\eta}\|
   +\sup_{v\in[0,\eta]}\|W_{\eta}-W_{\eta-v}\|.
$$
By stationary increments and time reversal, both suprema on the right hand side have the same distribution as $\sup_{v\in[0,\eta]}\|W_v\|$. Hence, by Minkowski's inequality,
$$
\|Y_0\|_{2p}\leq 2\left\Vert \sup_{v\in[0,\eta]}\|W_v\| \right\Vert _{2p}.
$$
Since $\|\cdot\|$ is convex and $W_u$ is a martingale, the process $M_u:=\|W_u\|$ is a nonnegative submartingale. Thus, by Doob's maximal inequality, for $p\geq 1$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:doob}
\|Y_0\|_{2p}
\leq 2\left\Vert \sup_{u\in[0,\eta]} M_u \right\Vert _{2p}
\leq 4\|M_\eta\|_{2p}
=4\|\,\|W_\eta\|\,\|_{2p}.
\end{equation}
Since $W_\eta\stackrel{d}{=}\sqrt{\eta}\,G$ with $G\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_d)$, standard Gaussian moment bounds imply that for $p\geq 2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gauss_moment}
\|\,\|W_\eta\|\,\|_{2p}^2
=\eta\,\|\,\|G\|\,\|_{2p}^2
\leq c_1\,\eta\, d\, p,
\end{equation}
for a universal constant $c_1>0$. Combining \eqref{eq:doob} and \eqref{eq:gauss_moment}, we obtain for $p\geq 2$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Y0_bound}
\|Y_0\|_{2p}^2 \leq c_2\,\eta\, d\, p,
\end{equation}
with a universal constant $c_2>0$. Plugging \eqref{eq:Y0_bound} into \eqref{eq:max_lp} gives
$$
\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leq i\leq N-1} Y_i^2\right]
\leq c_2\, N^{1/p}\,\eta\, d\, p.
$$
Choose $p:=\lceil \log N\rceil$. Since $T/\eta\geq e$, we have $N\geq3$ and hence $p\geq2$. Also $N^{1/p}\leq e$. Moreover, $N\leq T/\eta+1\leq2T/\eta$ and $\log(T/\eta)\geq1$, so
\begin{equation}
p\leq\log N+1
\leq\log(T/\eta)+\log2+1
\leq(2+\log2)\log(T/\eta).
\end{equation}
Thus,
$$
\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leq i\leq N-1} Y_i^2\right]
\leq c_3\, \eta\, d\, \log(T/\eta),
$$
for a universal constant $c_3>0$. Finally, using \eqref{eq:reduce_to_Y},
$$
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s,t\in[0,T],|t-s|\leq \eta}\|W_t-W_s\|^2\right]
\leq 4\,\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leq i\leq N-1} Y_i^2\right]
\leq C\, d\,\eta\log(T/\eta),
$$
where $C:=4c_3$ is a universal constant. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
We first estimate the continuous regulator $\hat K$. Since $\operatorname{dist}(X_0,\partial\mathcal C)\geq r_0$ almost surely, $B(X_0,r_0)\subset\mathcal C$ almost surely. For $\hat L(ds)$ almost every $s$, the point $X_0+r_0\hat\nu_s$ belongs to $\mathcal C$. Since $\hat\nu_s\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(\hat X_s)$, we have $\left\langle\hat\nu_s,X_0+r_0\hat\nu_s-\hat X_s\right\rangle\leq0$. Hence,
$$
\left\langle\hat X_s-X_0,\hat\nu_s\right\rangle
\geq r_0.
$$
And thus, we can get
\begin{align}
\int_0^t\left\langle\hat X_s-X_0,d\hat K_s\right\rangle
\geq r_0|\hat K|_t.\label{eq:hat:K:normal:lower}
\end{align}
Define
\begin{align}
\widehat D_t
&:=\int_0^t\left\langle\hat X_s-X_0, \, b\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)\right\rangle ds, \qquad
\widehat M_t:=\int_0^t\left\langle\hat X_s-X_0,
\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)dW_s\right\rangle,\nonumber\\
\widehat Q_t
&:=\int_0^t
\left\|\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2ds.
\end{align}
Since $\hat K$ has finite variation, the trace of the quadratic variation of $\hat X$ is $\widehat Q_t$. It\^o's formula, integrated from $0$ to $t$ with $\hat X_0=X_0$, therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|\hat X_t-X_0\right\|^2
=2\widehat D_t+2\widehat M_t
-2\int_0^t\left\langle\hat X_s-X_0,d\hat K_s\right\rangle
+\widehat Q_t.\label{eq:hat:K:Ito:integrated}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:hat:K:normal:lower} and \eqref{eq:hat:K:Ito:integrated},
\begin{align}
2r_0|\hat K|_t
&\leq2\widehat D_t+2\widehat M_t+\widehat Q_t
-\left\|\hat X_t-X_0\right\|^2
\leq2|\widehat D_t|+2|\widehat M_t|+\widehat Q_t.\label{eq:hat:K:pathwise}
\end{align}
Because $\hat X_s,X_0\in\mathcal C\subset B(0,R)$, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, It\^o's isometry, and \eqref{eqn:b:sigma:bdd} yield
\begin{align}
|\widehat D_t|^2
&\leq\left(\int_0^t2RB_bds\right)^2
=4R^2B_b^2t^2,\label{eq:hat:K:D}\\
\mathbb E|\widehat M_t|^2
&=2\mathbb E\int_0^t
\left\|\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)
\left(\hat X_s-X_0\right)\right\|^2ds\nonumber\\
&\leq2\mathbb E\int_0^t
\left\|\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)I_d\right\|_{\rm op}^2
\left\|\hat X_s-X_0\right\|^2ds
\leq8R^2S_\sigma^2t,\label{eq:hat:K:M}\\
\widehat Q_t^2
&\leq\left(\int_0^t2S_\sigma^2ds\right)^2
=4S_\sigma^4t^2.\label{eq:hat:K:Q}
\end{align}
Using $(a+b+c)^2\leq3(a^2+b^2+c^2)$ in \eqref{eq:hat:K:pathwise}, we conclude that
\begin{align}
\mathbb E|\hat K|_t^2
&\leq3r_0^{-2}\left(
\mathbb E|\widehat D_t|^2+\mathbb E|\widehat M_t|^2
+\frac14\mathbb E\widehat Q_t^2\right)\nonumber\\
&\leq3r_0^{-2}\left(4R^2B_b^2+S_\sigma^4\right)t^2
+24r_0^{-2}R^2S_\sigma^2t
:=At^2+Bt.\label{eq:hat:K:proof:final}
\end{align}
Now we compute the bound on the discretization grid. Let $t_j=j\eta$ and write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete:increment:proof}
u_j:=\eta b\left(\bar X_{t_{j-1}}\right)
+\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{t_{j-1}}\right)\Delta W_j,
\qquad
k_j:=\Delta\bar K_j,
\qquad
\Delta W_j:=W_{t_j}-W_{t_{j-1}}.
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:discrete:regulator:increment},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete:update:proof}
\bar X_{t_j}=\bar X_{t_{j-1}}+u_j-k_j,
\qquad
k_j\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(\bar X_{t_j}).
\end{equation}
On $\{k_j\neq0\}$, the point $X_0+r_0k_j/\|k_j\|$ belongs to $\mathcal C$. Hence, the definition of the outward normal cone implies
\begin{align}
\left\langle k_j,
X_0+r_0\frac{k_j}{\|k_j\|}-\bar X_{t_j}\right\rangle \leq 0,
\qquad
\left\langle\bar X_{t_j}-X_0,k_j\right\rangle
\geq r_0\|k_j\|.\label{eq:projection:interior:ball}
\end{align}
The same inequality is immediate when $k_j=0$. Expanding the square in \eqref{eq:discrete:update:proof} gives
\begin{align}
\left\|\bar X_{t_j}-X_0\right\|^2
=\left\|\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0\right\|^2
+2\left\langle\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0,u_j\right\rangle+\|u_j\|^2
-2\left\langle\bar X_{t_j}-X_0,k_j\right\rangle-\|k_j\|^2.\label{eq:discrete:energy:identity}
\end{align}
Summing~\eqref{eq:discrete:energy:identity} from $j=1$ to $k$, using $\bar X_0=X_0$, and applying~\eqref{eq:projection:interior:ball}, we obtain
\begin{align}
2r_0\sum_{j=1}^k\|k_j\|
\leq2\sum_{j=1}^k
\left\langle\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0,u_j\right\rangle
+\sum_{j=1}^k\|u_j\|^2
\leq2|D_T|+2|M_T|+Q_T,\label{eq:bar:K:pathwise}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align}
&D_T:=\sum_{j=1}^k\eta
\left\langle\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0,b\left(\bar X_{t_{j-1}}\right)\right\rangle,\\
&M_T:=\sum_{j=1}^k
\left\langle\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0,
\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{t_{j-1}}\right)\Delta W_j\right\rangle,\quad
Q_T:=\sum_{j=1}^k\|u_j\|^2.
\end{align}
Since $|\bar K|_T=\sum_{j=1}^k\|k_j\|$, the drift bound and the discrete It\^o isometry give
\begin{align}
|D_T|^2
&\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^k2RB_b\eta\right)^2
=4R^2B_b^2T^2,\label{eq:bar:K:D}\\
\mathbb E|M_T|^2
&=2\sum_{j=1}^k\eta\,
\mathbb E\left[
\sigma\left(\bar X_{t_{j-1}}\right)^2
\left\|\bar X_{t_{j-1}}-X_0\right\|^2\right]
\leq8R^2S_\sigma^2T.\label{eq:bar:K:M}
\end{align}
For the last term, \eqref{eqn:b:sigma:bdd} implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bar:K:Q:pathwise}
Q_T
\leq2B_b^2T\eta
+\frac{4S_\sigma^2}{d}Q_W,
\qquad
Q_W:=\sum_{j=1}^k\|\Delta W_j\|^2.
\end{equation}
Since $Q_W/\eta$ has the chi squared distribution with $kd$ degrees of freedom,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:QW:second:moment}
\mathbb E\left[Q_W^2\right]
=\eta^2\bigl((kd)^2+2kd\bigr)
=d^2T^2+2dT\eta.
\end{equation}
Using $(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$, $d\geq1$, and $0<\eta\leq1$ in \eqref{eq:bar:K:Q:pathwise} yields
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left[Q_T^2\right]
\leq8B_b^4T^2\eta^2
+\frac{32S_\sigma^4}{d^2}\mathbb E Q_W^2
\leq\left(8B_b^4+32S_\sigma^4\right)T^2+64S_\sigma^4T.
\label{eq:bar:K:Q}
\end{align}
Finally,~\eqref{eq:bar:K:pathwise} and $(a+b+c)^2\leq3(a^2+b^2+c^2)$ give
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left[|\bar K|_T^2\right]
&\leq3r_0^{-2}\left(
\mathbb E|D_T|^2+\mathbb E|M_T|^2+\frac14\mathbb E Q_T^2
\right)\nonumber\\
&\leq3r_0^{-2}\left(4R^2B_b^2+2B_b^4+8S_\sigma^4\right)T^2
+3r_0^{-2}\left(8R^2S_\sigma^2+16S_\sigma^4\right)T
=\bar A T^2+\bar B T.
\end{align}
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Denote $r(s):=\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta$. For $0\leq t\leq T$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bridge:DeltaY}
\Delta_Y(t):=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left\|\bar Y_s-\hat Y_s\right\|.
\end{equation}
The convex Skorokhod comparison estimate, with the outward regulator convention $X=Y-K$, gives, for every $t\leq T$,
\begin{align}
\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2
\leq\left\|\bar Y_t-\hat Y_t\right\|^2
-2\int_0^t
\left\langle
\left(\bar Y_t-\hat Y_t\right)-\left(\bar Y_s-\hat Y_s\right),
d\left(\bar K_s-\hat K_s\right)
\right\rangle.\label{eq:tanaka:bridge}
\end{align}
This is the convex Skorokhod comparison of \cite{tanaka1979stochastic}, used for penalization in \cite[Eq.~(4.3)]{slominski2013penalization}. Denote $D=\bar Y-\hat Y$, $V=\bar K-\hat K$, and $E=D-V=\bar X-\hat X$. Since $V_0=0$, the bounded variation identity with values after each jump gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\|E_t\|^2&=\|D_t\|^2-2\langle D_t,V_t\rangle+\|V_t\|^2\\
&=\|D_t\|^2-2\int_0^t\langle D_t-D_s,dV_s\rangle-2\int_0^t\langle E_s,dV_s\rangle-\sum_{s\leq t}\|\Delta V_s\|^2.
\end{aligned}
$$
The normal cone conditions imply $\left\langle\bar X_s-\hat X_s,d\bar K_s\right\rangle\geq0$ and $\left\langle\bar X_s-\hat X_s,d\hat K_s\right\rangle\leq0$, so that $\int_0^t\langle E_s,dV_s\rangle\geq0$. Dropping this nonpositive contribution and the negative jump sum proves~\eqref{eq:tanaka:bridge}. By the definition of $\Delta_Y(t)$,
\begin{align}
2\left|\int_0^t
\left\langle
\left(\bar Y_t-\hat Y_t\right)-\left(\bar Y_s-\hat Y_s\right),
d\left(\bar K_s-\hat K_s\right)
\right\rangle\right|\nonumber
&\leq
4\Delta_Y(t)\operatorname{Var}_{[0,t]}\left(\bar K-\hat K\right)\nonumber\\
&\leq4\Delta_Y(t)\left(|\bar K|_t+|\hat K|_t\right).
\label{eq:tanaka:bridge:variation}
\end{align}
Taking the supremum in~\eqref{eq:tanaka:bridge} and using \eqref{eq:tanaka:bridge:variation} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:slominski:bridge:comparison}
\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left\|\bar X_s-\hat X_s\right\|^2
\leq\Delta^2_Y(T)
+4\Delta_Y(t)\left(|\bar K|_t+|\hat K|_t\right).
\end{equation}
The inequality applies to the c\`adl\`ag driver $\bar Y$ and the continuous driver $\hat Y$. In particular, it retains the contribution of $\bar K$. Since $\bar Y_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}=\hat Y_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}$ and the coefficients of $\hat Y$ are constant on $\left[\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta,s\right]$,
\begin{align}
\bar Y_s-\hat Y_s
&=-\int_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}^s b\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)du
-\int_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}^s\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)dW_u\nonumber\\
&=-\left(s-\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta\right)b\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)
-\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)\left(W_s-W_{\lfloor s/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right).
\label{eq:bridge:Y:difference}
\end{align}
Therefore, using $(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$ and \eqref{eqn:b:sigma:bdd},
\begin{align}
\Delta^2_Y(T)
&\leq2B_b^2\eta^2
+4S_\sigma^2\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}
\left\|W_s-W_{r(s)}\right\|^2.\label{eq:bridge:Y:pathwise}
\end{align}
Lemma~\ref{lemma:bound:bm} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bridge:Y:L2}
\mathbb E\left[\Delta^2_Y(T)\right]
\leq2B_b^2\eta^2+4CdS_\sigma^2\eta\log(T/\eta).
\end{equation}
Taking expectations in~\eqref{eq:slominski:bridge:comparison} at $t=T$ and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice yield
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}
\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
&\leq\mathbb E\left[\Delta^2_Y(T)\right]
+4\mathbb E\left[\Delta_Y(T)|\bar K|_T\right]
+4\mathbb E\left[\Delta_Y(T)|\hat K|_T\right]\nonumber\\
&\leq2B_b^2\eta^2+4CdS_\sigma^2\eta\log(T/\eta)\nonumber\\
&\quad+4\left(2B_b^2\eta^2
+4CdS_\sigma^2\eta\log(T/\eta)\right)^{1/2}
\cdot\left(\sqrt{\bar A T^2+\bar B T}
+\sqrt{AT^2+BT}\right).
\label{eq:bridge:first:bound}
\end{align}
Since $T\geq e$,
\begin{align}
\sqrt{AT^2+BT}
&\leq T\left(\sqrt A+\sqrt{B/T}\right)
\leq T\left(\sqrt A+\sqrt{B/e}\right),\nonumber\\
\sqrt{\bar A T^2+\bar B T}
&\leq T\left(\sqrt{\bar A}+\sqrt{\bar B/e}\right).
\label{eq:bridge:regulator:simplification}
\end{align}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\left(2B_b^2\eta^2
+4CdS_\sigma^2\eta\log(T/\eta)\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt2B_b\eta
+2\sqrt{Cd}\,S_\sigma\sqrt{\eta\log(T/\eta)}.
\label{eq:bridge:Y:L2:square:root}
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:bridge:regulator:simplification} and \eqref{eq:bridge:Y:L2:square:root} into \eqref{eq:bridge:first:bound} gives
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}
\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
&\leq2B_b^2\eta^2+4CdS_\sigma^2\eta\log(T/\eta)\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad+4TH_K\left(
\sqrt2B_b\eta
+2\sqrt{Cd}\,S_\sigma\sqrt{\eta\log(T/\eta)}
\right).
\label{eq:bridge:before:uniform}
\end{align}
To collect these terms, note that $0<\eta\leq1$, $T\geq e$, and $\log(T/\eta)\geq1$ imply
$$
\begin{aligned}
\eta^2&\leq\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta),\qquad \eta\log(T/\eta)\leq\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta),\\
T\eta&\leq\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta),\qquad T\sqrt{\eta\log(T/\eta)}\leq\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta).
\end{aligned}
$$
Applying these inequalities term by term in~\eqref{eq:bridge:before:uniform} gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
&\leq\left[2B_b^2+4CdS_\sigma^2+4H_K\left(\sqrt2B_b+2\sqrt{Cd}\,S_\sigma\right)\right]\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta)\\
&=C_0\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta),
\end{aligned}
$$
where the last equality is~\eqref{eq:bridge:C0}. This proves~\eqref{eq:bridge:uniform}.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Recall that $K$ and $\hat K$ are outward regulators of bounded variation on every finite time interval in the decompositions $X=Y-K$ and $\hat X=\hat Y-\hat K$. Let $L(dt):=d|K|_t$ and $\hat L(dt):=d|\hat K|_t$ be their total variation measures. The polar decomposition of the vector measures $dK$ and $d\hat K$ gives measurable vector fields $\nu$ and $\hat\nu$ such that
\begin{equation}
dK_t=\nu_tL(dt),
\qquad
d\hat K_t=\hat\nu_t\hat L(dt),
\label{eq:theorem:reflection:polar}
\end{equation}
with $\|\nu_t\|=1$ for $L(dt)$ almost every $t$ and $\|\hat\nu_t\|=1$ for $\hat L(dt)$ almost every $t$. The defining properties of the two Skorokhod problems further give
$$
\nu_t\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(X_t)
\quad L(dt)\text{-almost everywhere},
\qquad
\hat\nu_t\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(\hat X_t)
\quad \hat L(dt)\text{-almost everywhere},
$$
and $L$ and $\hat L$ are supported on the times at which $X$ and $\hat X$ lie on $\partial\mathcal C$, respectively. This establishes \eqref{eq:theorem:reflection:polar} from the defining properties of the two Skorokhod decompositions.

We now verify the sign of each reflection term. For $L(dt)$ almost every $t$, one has $\nu_t\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(X_t)$. Since $\hat X_t\in\mathcal C$, substitute $y=\hat X_t$ into \eqref{eq:outward:normal:cone} to obtain
\begin{align}
\left\langle X_t-\hat X_t,\nu_t\right\rangle\geq0
\qquad L(dt)\text{-almost everywhere}.
\label{eq:theorem:reflection:first:sign}
\end{align}
For $\hat L(dt)$ almost every $t$, one has $\hat\nu_t\in N_{\mathcal C}^{\rm out}(\hat X_t)$. Since $X_t\in\mathcal C$, substitute $y=X_t$ into \eqref{eq:outward:normal:cone} to obtain
\begin{align}
\left\langle X_t-\hat X_t,\hat\nu_t\right\rangle\leq0
\qquad \hat L(dt)\text{-almost everywhere}.
\label{eq:theorem:reflection:second:sign}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:theorem:reflection:polar}-\eqref{eq:theorem:reflection:second:sign} yields
\begin{align}
\int_0^t\left\langle X_s-\hat X_s,d\left(K_s-\hat K_s\right)\right\rangle
&=\int_0^t\left\langle X_s-\hat X_s,\nu_s\right\rangle L(ds)-\int_0^t\left\langle X_s-\hat X_s,\hat\nu_s\right\rangle\hat L(ds)\geq0.\label{Skorokhod:ineqn}
\end{align}
By~\eqref{simplified eqn:RALD} and~\eqref{eqn:hat:x},
\begin{equation}
d\hat{X}_t = b\left(\bar{X}_{\lfloor t/\eta \rfloor\eta}\right)dt + \sqrt{2}\sigma\left(\bar{X}_{\lfloor t/\eta \rfloor\eta}\right)dW_t - d\hat{K}_t, \qquad \hat{X}_0=X_0,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
d{X}_t = b({X}_t)dt + \sqrt{2}\sigma({X}_t)dW_t - d{K}_t.
\end{equation}
Both $X$ and $\hat X$ are continuous semimartingales. Set $Z_t:=X_t-\hat X_t$ and $r(t):=\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor\eta$. Then
\begin{align}
dZ_t
&=\left[b(X_t)-b(\hat X_t)+b(\hat X_t)-b\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]dt
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad\qquad+\sqrt2\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)
+\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]dW_t
-d\left(K_t-\hat K_t\right).
\end{align}
It\^o's formula gives
\begin{align}
d\|Z_t\|^2
&=2\left\langle Z_t,
b(X_t)-b(\hat X_t)+b(\hat X_t)-b\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right\rangle dt
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad+2\sqrt2\left\langle Z_t,
\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)
+\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]dW_t\right\rangle
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad\qquad+2\left\|\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)
+\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2dt
-2\left\langle Z_t,d\left(K_t-\hat K_t\right)\right\rangle
\nonumber
\\
&\leq2\left\langle Z_t,b(X_t)-b(\hat X_t)\right\rangle dt
+\left(\|Z_t\|^2+\left\|b(\hat X_t)-b\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right\|^2\right)dt
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad+2\sqrt2\left\langle Z_t,
\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)
+\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]dW_t\right\rangle
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad\qquad+4\left\|\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)\right]I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2dt
+4\left\|\left[\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2dt,
\label{eq:ito:X:hat:X}
\end{align}
where~\eqref{Skorokhod:ineqn}, $2\langle u,v\rangle\leq
\|u\|^2+\|v\|^2$, and $2\|A+B\|_{\rm F}^2\leq
4\|A\|_{\rm F}^2+4\|B\|_{\rm F}^2$ were used. Now by assumptions, we can have
\begin{align}
2\left\langle Z_t,b(X_t)-b(\hat X_t)\right\rangle
&\leq-2m\|Z_t\|^2,\qquad
\left\|b(\hat X_t)-b\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right\|^2 \leq M^2\left\|\hat X_t-\bar X_{r(t)}\right\|^2,\nonumber\\
4\left\|\left[\sigma(X_t)-\sigma(\hat X_t)\right]I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2
&\leq4\alpha\|Z_t\|^2,\qquad
4\left\|\left[\sigma(\hat X_t)-\sigma\left(\bar X_{r(t)}\right)\right]I_d\right\|_{\rm F}^2\leq4\alpha\left\|\hat X_t-\bar X_{r(t)}\right\|^2.
\label{eq:global:coefficient:bounds}
\end{align}
Let $q:=2m-1-4\alpha>0$ and $c:=M^2+4\alpha$. Apply the product formula to $e^{qt}\|Z_t\|^2$ in \eqref{eq:ito:X:hat:X}, using \eqref{eq:global:coefficient:bounds}. The reflection contribution $-2e^{qt}\langle Z_t,d(K_t-\hat K_t)\rangle$ is a nonpositive measure by the two pointwise normal cone inequalities above. The stochastic integral is square integrable on every fixed interval $[0,T]$, since $Z$, $\sigma$, and $e^{qt}$ are bounded there. Integrating, taking expectations, and using $Z_0=0$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eqn:lyapunov}
e^{qT}\mathbb E\|Z_T\|^2
\leq c\int_0^T e^{qs}
\mathbb E\left\|\hat X_s-\bar X_{r(s)}\right\|^2\,ds.
\end{equation}
Since $\bar X$ is constant on $[r(t),r(t)+\eta)$, $\bar X_{r(t)}=\bar X_t$. Lemma~\ref{lemma:alg2bri} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bar:X:hat:X}
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathbb E\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2
\leq\mathbb E\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}
\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
\leq C_0\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta),
\end{equation}
where $C_0$ is defined in~\eqref{eq:bridge:C0}. Dividing \eqref{eqn:lyapunov} by $e^{qT}$ and using $\int_0^T e^{-q(T-s)}ds\leq1/q$ gives
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\|Z_T\|^2
\leq c\int_0^T e^{-q(T-s)}
\mathbb E\left\|\hat X_s-\bar X_s\right\|^2ds
\leq\frac{c}{q}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}
\mathbb E\left\|\hat X_s-\bar X_s\right\|^2.
\label{eq:global:gronwall}
\end{align}
Combining~\eqref{eq:global:gronwall} with~\eqref{eq:bar:X:hat:X} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:X:hatX:uniform}
\mathbb E\left\|X_T-\hat X_T\right\|^2
\leq \frac{M^2+4\alpha}{2m-1-4\alpha}
C_0\sqrt\eta\,T\log(T/\eta).
\end{equation}
Finally, Minkowski's inequality in $L^2$ and \eqref{eq:bar:X:hat:X}-\eqref{eq:X:hatX:uniform} give
\begin{align}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_T),\mathrm{Law}(X_T)\right)
&\leq \left(\mathbb E\left\|\bar X_T-X_T\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\leq \left(\mathbb E\left\|\bar X_T-\hat X_T\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}
+\left(\mathbb E\left\|\hat X_T-X_T\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}\nonumber\\
&\leq \left(1+\sqrt{\frac{M^2+4\alpha}{2m-1-4\alpha}}\right)
\sqrt{C_0\,T\log(T/\eta)}\,\eta^{1/4}.
\end{align}
Squaring this inequality proves the result. The proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Fix $T=k\eta\geq e$. By Corollary~\ref{cor:W:2},
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathrm{Law}(X_{k\eta}),\pi)\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{0} k\eta/(2C_{P})},
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_{0}$ and $C_{P}$ are defined in Theorem~\ref{thm:chi:square}. Since $2m>1+4\alpha$, Theorem~\ref{global:error:2} gives
\begin{align}
\mathcal{W}_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar{X}_{k\eta}),\mathrm{Law}(X_{k\eta})\right)  & \leq \eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}\left(\log(k)\right)^{1/2}
\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{M^2+4\alpha}{2m-1-4\alpha}}\right)\sqrt{C_0},
\end{align}
where $C_{0}$ is given in Theorem~\ref{global:error:2}. Finally, by using the triangle inequality for $\mathcal W_2$ distance and the fact that $\mathrm{Law}(\bar{X}_{k\eta})=\mathrm{Law}(x_{k})$, we conclude that
\begin{align}
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathrm{Law}(x_{k}),\pi)
&\leq
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathrm{Law}(X_{k\eta}),\pi)
+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathrm{Law}(x_{k}),\mathrm{Law}(X_{k\eta}))
\nonumber
\\
&\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^{2}(\mu_{0}\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{0} k\eta/(2C_{P})}
+\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}\left(\log(k)\right)^{1/2}\bar{C}_{0},
\end{align}
where $\bar{C}_{0}$ is defined in \eqref{eq:thm:final:Cbar}. This proves \eqref{eq:thm:final:explicit}.

Suppose now that $\sigma\equiv1$ and that $B_b,M,m,R,r_0$ are independent of $d$. Then $\alpha=0$ and $S_\sigma=\|I_d\|_{\mathrm F}=\sqrt d$. The definitions in Lemmas~\ref{lemma:bound:K} and~\ref{lemma:alg2bri} give
$$
\begin{gathered}
A=\mathcal O(d^2),\quad B=\mathcal O(d),\quad
\bar A=\mathcal O(d^2),\quad \bar B=\mathcal O(d^2),\quad
H_K=\mathcal O(d),\quad C_0=\mathcal O(d^2).
\end{gathered}
$$
Since $M$ and $m$ are independent of $d$, \eqref{eq:thm:final:Cbar} yields
$$
\bar C_0
=\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{M^2}{2m-1}}\right)\sqrt{C_0} =\mathcal O(d).
$$
Substituting this estimate into \eqref{eq:thm:final:explicit} proves \eqref{eq:thm:final:dimension}. The proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Let $A_0$ and $T_\varepsilon$ be as in the statement. The definition of $T_\varepsilon$ gives
$$
    \frac{\alpha_0T_\varepsilon}{2C_P}
    \geq\log\left(1 \vee \frac{2A_0}{\varepsilon}\right).
$$
Consequently,
$$
    A_0\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_0T_\varepsilon}{2C_P}\right)
    \leq\frac{A_0}{\max\left\{1,2A_0/\varepsilon\right\}}
    \leq\frac\varepsilon2.
$$

Choose $k$ as in \eqref{eq:complexity:valid:k} and set $\eta=T_\varepsilon/k$. Then $0<\eta\leq1$ and $k\eta=T_\varepsilon\geq e$, so Theorem~\ref{thm:final} applies. The fourth power of its discretization term is
$$
\begin{aligned}
    \left(\bar C_0\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2}\right)^4
    &=\bar C_0^4\frac{T_\varepsilon}{k}T_\varepsilon^2(\log k)^2=\frac{\bar C_0^4T_\varepsilon^3(\log k)^2}{k}
    \leq\frac{\varepsilon^4}{16}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Taking fourth roots shows that the discretization term is at most $\varepsilon/2$. Therefore
$$
    \mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(x_k),\pi)
    \leq\frac\varepsilon2+\frac\varepsilon2
    =\varepsilon.
$$

Finally, the function $k/(\log k)^2$ tends to infinity. The smallest integer satisfying \eqref{eq:complexity:valid:k} is bounded, up to logarithmic factors, by
$$
    T_\varepsilon+\frac{\bar C_0^4T_\varepsilon^3}{\varepsilon^4}.
$$
Since $T_\varepsilon$ grows at most logarithmically in $1/\varepsilon$ when the remaining parameters are fixed, this proves the stated complexity. Under the additional scaling assumptions in Theorem~\ref{thm:final}, the second part of that theorem gives $\bar C_0=\mathcal O(d)$, which yields the $d^4$ dependence in \eqref{under:additional}. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Fix an interior ball $B(z,r)\subset\mathcal C$, and write
$$
D:=\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C), \quad S:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal C}\|\sigma(x)I_d\|_{\mathrm F}.
$$
Use the bridge $\hat X$ from \eqref{eqn:hat:x} with $b=0$. The regulator estimates in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lemma:bound:K} use only bounded coefficients and an interior ball. To allow every initial point, expand the squared distance to $z$, rather than to $X_0$. The initial squared distance is then at most $D^2$. The same continuous and discrete energy identities give
$$
r|\hat K|_s\leq D^2/2+|\hat M_s|+S^2s,\qquad r|\bar K|_s\leq D^2/2+|\bar M_s|+\tfrac12 Q_s,
$$
Here, with $\Delta W_{j+1}:=W_{(j+1)\eta}-W_{j\eta}$, the martingale terms are
$$
\begin{aligned}
\hat M_s:=\int_0^s\left\langle\hat X_u-z,
\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{\lfloor u/\eta\rfloor\eta}\right)\,dW_u\right\rangle,\quad \bar M_s:=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\left\langle\bar X_{j\eta}-z,
\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{j\eta}\right)\Delta W_{j+1}\right\rangle,
\end{aligned}
$$
and 
\[
Q_s:=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\left\|\sqrt2\sigma\left(\bar X_{j\eta}\right)\Delta W_{j+1}\right\|^2.
\]
Since the states lie in $\mathcal C$ and the coefficients are bounded, It\^o's isometry and orthogonality of the discrete martingale increments give $\mathbb E|\hat M_s|^2,\mathbb E|\bar M_s|^2\leq2D^2S^2s$. Since $\sigma$ is scalar,
$$
Q_s\leq\frac{2S^2}{d}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\|\Delta W_{j+1}\|^2,\qquad
\mathbb E Q_s^2\leq4S^4(s^2+2s\eta/d)\leq12S^4s^2.
$$
Consequently,
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left[|\hat K|_s^2\right]&\leq
R_{\rm c}(s):=3r^{-2}\left(D^4/4+2D^2S^2s+S^4s^2\right),\nonumber\\
\mathbb E\left[|\bar K|_s^2\right]&\leq
R_{\rm d}(s):=3r^{-2}\left(D^4/4+2D^2S^2s+3S^4s^2\right).
\label{eq:const:regulator:bounds}
\end{align}
These bounds are uniform over $X_0\in\mathcal C$, in particular, they do not require the initial law to avoid the boundary. The Brownian maximum argument of Lemma~\ref{lemma:bound:bm}, applied on the $n$ grid intervals, yields
$$
\mathbb E\left[\max_{0\leq j<n}\sup_{j\eta\leq t\leq(j+1)\eta}
\|W_t-W_{j\eta}\|^2\right]\leq C_{\rm B}d\eta(1+\log n)
$$
for a universal $C_{\rm B}$. Indeed, the one dimensional reflection bound, followed by a union bound over intervals and integration of the tail, gives $C_{\rm B}\eta(1 + \log n)$ per coordinate. This version holds for every $s>0$. Thus,
$$
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{t\leq s}\left\|\bar Y_t-\hat Y_t\right\|^2\right]\leq\beta\eta(1+\log n),\qquad \beta:=2C_{\rm B}S^2.
$$
The convex Skorokhod comparison \eqref{eq:slominski:bridge:comparison} and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality give
$$
\mathbb E\left[\sup_{t\leq s}\left\|\bar X_t-\hat X_t\right\|^2\right]
\leq\beta\eta(1+\log n)+4\sqrt{\beta\eta(1+\log n)}\,V_s,\qquad
V_s:=\sqrt{R_{\rm c}(s)}+\sqrt{R_{\rm d}(s)}.
$$
Since $(1 + \log n)\leq n$, we have $\eta(1+\log n)\leq n\eta=s$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:Is}
G_s:=\beta\sqrt s+4\sqrt\beta\,V_s,\qquad
I_s:=\left(1+\sqrt{e^{4\ell_\sigma^2s}-1}\right)\sqrt{G_s}.
\end{equation}
The bridge error is at most $G_s\sqrt{\eta(1+\log n)}$. For $f(t)=\mathbb E\|X_t-\hat X_t\|^2$, the reflection sign already proved in \eqref{Skorokhod:ineqn}, It\^o's formula, and coefficient Lipschitz continuity imply
$$
f(t)\leq4\ell_\sigma^2\int_0^t\left(f(u)+G_s\sqrt{\eta(1+\log n)}\right)\,du,\qquad t\leq s.
$$
Therefore, Gr\"{o}nwall's inequlity gives
\[
f(s)\leq\left(e^{4\ell_\sigma^2s}-1\right)G_s\sqrt{\eta(1+\log n)}.
\]
This formula also holds when $\ell_\sigma=0$, since then $f(s)=0$. The synchronous construction and the $L^2$ triangle inequality now yield
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_s),\mathrm{Law}(X_s)\right)
&\leq\left(\mathbb E\left\|\bar X_s-\hat X_s\right\|^2\right)^{1/2}+\left(f(s)\right)^{1/2}\\
&\leq\left(1+\sqrt{e^{4\ell_\sigma^2s}-1}\right)\sqrt{G_s}[\eta(1+\log n)]^{1/4}\\
&:=I_s[\eta(1+\log(s/\eta))]^{1/4}.
\end{aligned}
$$
The estimates are uniform over the initial point in $\mathcal C$. Conditioning on any common initial law and integrating preserves the same bound, proving~\eqref{eq:const:strong:euler:log-rate}.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Synchronous coupling, the convex reflection sign, and It\^o's formula give $\mathbb E\|X_t^x-X_t^y\|^2\leq e^{2\ell_\sigma^2t}\|x-y\|^2$. Integrating over a coupling of the initial laws proves \eqref{eq:const:short:stability}. For smooth $u$, integration along the segment in $\mathcal C$ and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality give
$$
|P_su(y)-P_su(x)|\leq\bigl(\mathbb E\|X_s^y-X_s^x\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}\left(\mathbb E\int_0^1\left\|\nabla u\left((1-\theta)X_s^x+\theta X_s^y\right)\right\|^2d\theta\right)^{1/2}.
$$
The mean square bound gives $X_s^y\to X_s^x$ in probability as $y\to x$. Dividing by $\|y-x\|$ and using bounded uniform continuity of $\nabla u$ yields
$$
\limsup_{\substack{y\to x\\y\in\mathcal C,\ y\neq x}}
\frac{|P_su(y)-P_su(x)|}{\|y-x\|}
\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\bigl[P_s(|\nabla u|^2)(x)\bigr]^{1/2}.
$$
The kernel bound $\kappa_s\leq M_s$ and integration of local slopes along segments yield
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:H1:smoothing}
\operatorname{Lip}(P_su)\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\sqrt{M_s}\,
\|\nabla u\|_{L^2(\pi)}.
\end{equation}
Smooth approximation in $H^1(\mathcal C)$ and $\|P_sv\|_\infty\leq\sqrt{M_s}\|v\|_2$ extend \eqref{eq:const:H1:smoothing} to $u\in H^1(\mathcal C)$. Spectral calculus for $-\mathcal L$ from Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure}~(ii) gives, for $\pi(f)=0$ and $v\geq0$,
$$
\mathcal E(P_{s+v}f,P_{s+v}f)
\leq\frac{1}{2es}\|P_vf\|_2^2
\leq\frac{e^{-2g_0v}}{2es}\|f\|_2^2,
$$
because $\sup_{z\geq0}ze^{-2sz}=1/(2es)$ and the spectral gap is at least $g_0$. Using $\mathcal E(u,u)\geq\lambda\|\nabla u\|_2^2$ in \eqref{eq:const:H1:smoothing}, we obtain
$$
\operatorname{Lip}(P_tf)
\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\sqrt{\frac{M_s}{2es\lambda}}\,
e^{-g_0(t-2s)}\|f\|_2,\qquad t\geq2s.
$$
To pass from test functions to densities, first note that the heat kernel bound makes $\mu P_t$ and $\nu P_t$ have bounded densities for $t\geq s$. Their difference has zero integral. For any mean zero $f\in L^2(\pi)$ with $\|f\|_2\leq1$,
$$
\left|\int_{\mathcal C}f\,d(\mu P_t-\nu P_t)\right|=\left|\int_{\mathcal C}P_tf\,d(\mu-\nu)\right|\leq\operatorname{Lip}(P_tf)\mathcal W_1(\mu,\nu).
$$
Taking the supremum over these test functions gives the $L^2(\pi)$ norm of the density difference. Combining the preceding Lipschitz estimate with $\mathcal W_1(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu)$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:const:density:stability}
\left\|\frac{d(\mu P_t-\nu P_t)}{d\pi}\right\|_2
\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\sqrt{\frac{M_s}{2es\lambda}}\,
e^{-g_0(t-2s)}\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu).
\end{equation}
For probability densities $a,b\in L^2(\pi)$ with $a,b\geq m>0$, solve the weak Neumann equation
$$
\int_{\mathcal{C}}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla v\,d\pi=\int_{\mathcal{C}}(b-a)v\,d\pi,\qquad \pi(\psi)=0.
$$
On the mean zero subspace of $H^1(\mathcal C)$, the Poincar\'e inequality makes the gradient form coercive, so Lax-Milgram~\cite[Corollary~5.8]{brezis2011functional} gives a unique mean zero solution. Taking $v=\psi$ in its weak equation gives
$$
\|\nabla\psi\|_2^2=\int_{\mathcal C}(b-a)\psi\,d\pi\leq\|b-a\|_2\|\psi\|_2\leq\sqrt{C_P}\|b-a\|_2\|\nabla\psi\|_2.
$$
Dividing when $\|\nabla\psi\|_2>0$, with the zero case immediate, yields $\|\nabla\psi\|_2^2\leq C_P\|a-b\|_2^2$. For the density path $a_\theta=(1-\theta)a+\theta b$, the flux $(d\pi/dx)\nabla\psi$ satisfies the weak continuity equation with zero boundary flux. The Benamou-Brenier action bound~\cite{benamou2000computational} gives
$$
\mathcal W_2^2(a\pi,b\pi)
\leq\int_0^1\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^2}{a_\theta}\,d\pi\,d\theta
\leq\frac{C_P}{m}\|a-b\|_2^2.
$$
This weak formulation requires no differentiability of $d\pi/dx$, see \cite[Section~2]{peyre2018comparison}. For $t\geq s$, the semigroup property gives $m_s\leq\kappa_t\leq M_s$, so the densities of $\mu P_t,\nu P_t$ lie in $L^2(\pi)$ and are bounded below by $m_s$ for arbitrary initial laws. Applying the transport estimate to the densities of $\mu P_t$ and $\nu P_t$, then using~\eqref{eq:const:density:stability}, gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2(\mu P_t,\nu P_t)&\leq\sqrt{\frac{C_P}{m_s}}\left\|\frac{d(\mu P_t-\nu P_t)}{d\pi}\right\|_2\\
&\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\sqrt{\frac{C_PM_s}{2es\lambda m_s}}e^{-g_0(t-2s)}\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu)\\
&=H_se^{-g_0(t-2s)}\mathcal W_2(\mu,\nu),
\end{aligned}
$$
since $g_0=\lambda/C_P$ and $H_s$ is defined in~\eqref{eq:const:heatkernel:constants}. This proves~\eqref{eq:const:long:stability}.
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Let $Q_\eta$ denote one projected Euler-Maruyama step and $Q=Q_\eta^n$. Proposition~\ref{prop:const:strong:euler}, conditioned on the initial state, gives for every probability measure $\nu$ on $\mathcal C$
$$
\mathcal W_2(\nu Q,\nu P_s)\leq
\delta_s:=I_s[\eta(1+\log(s/\eta))]^{1/4}.
$$
Set $\rho_i:=\mu_0Q^iP_{(m-i)s}$ for $i=0,\ldots,m$, so that $\rho_0=\mu_0P_{ms}$ and $\rho_m=\mu_0Q^m$. For $i=1,\ldots,m$, write $\nu_i:=\mu_0Q^{i-1}$ and $j=m-i$. The semigroup property gives
$$
\rho_i=(\nu_iQ)P_{js},\qquad
\rho_{i-1}=(\nu_iP_s)P_{js}.
$$
Since $\mathcal W_2(\nu_iQ,\nu_iP_s)\leq\delta_s$, Lemma~\ref{lem:const:semigroup:stability} yields
$$
\mathcal W_2(\rho_i,\rho_{i-1})
\leq\delta_s
\begin{cases}
1,&j=0,\\
e^{\ell_\sigma^2s},&j=1,\\
H_se^{-g_0(j-2)s},&j\geq2.
\end{cases}
$$
For $m\geq3$, the triangle inequality and the change of index $j=m-i$ give
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mu_0Q^m,\mu_0P_{ms}\right)&\leq\sum_{i=1}^m\mathcal W_2(\rho_i,\rho_{i-1})\\
&\leq\delta_s\left(1+e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}+H_s\sum_{j=2}^{m-1}e^{-g_0(j-2)s}\right)\\
&\leq\delta_s\left(1+e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}+H_s\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}e^{-g_0(j-2)s}\right)\\
&=\delta_s\left(1+e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}+\frac{H_s}{1-e^{-g_0s}}\right)=B_s\delta_s.
\end{aligned}
$$
For $m=1$, only $j=0$ occurs, while for $m=2$ only $j=0,1$ occur. The same triangle inequality therefore gives
$$
\mathcal W_2\left(\mu_0Q,\mu_0P_s\right)\leq\delta_s\leq B_s\delta_s,\quad
\mathcal W_2\left(\mu_0Q^2,\mu_0P_{2s}\right)\leq\delta_s\left(1+e^{\ell_\sigma^2s}\right)\leq B_s\delta_s.
$$
Thus the estimate holds for every integer $m\geq1$. To identify the numerical law, use the one step transition kernel $Q_\eta$ and the initial law $\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_0)=\mu_0$. For every Borel set $A\subseteq\mathcal C$ and integer $k\geq0$, the Markov property gives
$$
\mathbb P(\bar X_{(k+1)\eta}\in A)=\int_{\mathcal C}Q_\eta(x,A)\,\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{k\eta})(dx).
$$
Induction yields $\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{k\eta})=\mu_0Q_\eta^k$. Since $Q=Q_\eta^n$ and $n\eta=s$,
$$
\mu_0Q^m=\mu_0(Q_\eta^n)^m=\mu_0Q_\eta^{nm}=\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{nm\eta})=\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{ms}).
$$
Substituting this identity, $T=ms$, the definition of $\delta_s$, and $F_s=B_sI_s$ into the preceding estimate gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2\left(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_T),\mu_0P_T\right)&=\mathcal W_2\left(\mu_0Q^m,\mu_0P_{ms}\right)\\
&\leq B_s\delta_s=B_sI_s\left[\eta\left(1+\log\frac{s}{\eta}\right)\right]^{1/4}
=F_s\left[\eta\left(1+\log\frac{s}{\eta}\right)\right]^{1/4},
\end{aligned}
$$
which proves~\eqref{eq:const:uniform:comparison}.
Under the additional initial $\chi^2$ condition, Corollary~\ref{cor:W:2}, $\alpha_0\geq\lambda$, and $g_0=\lambda/C_P$ give
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2(\mathrm{Law}(\bar X_{ms}),\pi)
&\leq\mathcal W_2(\mu_0Q^m,\mu_0P_{ms}) +\mathcal W_2(\mu_0P_{ms},\pi)\leq B_s\delta_s+A_0e^{-g_0ms/2}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Substituting $F_s=B_sI_s$ and $T=ms$ proves \eqref{eq:const-ref-unconditional-W2}.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
The chosen lower bound on $m$ gives
$$
A_0e^{-g_0ms/2}\leq\frac{A_0}{1+2A_0/\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon A_0}{\varepsilon+2A_0}\leq\frac\varepsilon2.
$$ 
Since $\eta=s/n$, the fourth power of the discretization term is
$$
F_s^4\eta(1+\log(s/\eta))=F_s^4s\,\frac{(1 + \log n)}n\leq\frac{\varepsilon^4}{16}.
$$
Thus the two terms in \eqref{eq:const-ref-unconditional-W2} sum to at most $\varepsilon$. For fixed $s$, choose $m$ to be the larger of $1$ and the ceiling of its stated lower bound, so $m=\mathcal O(1+\log(1/\varepsilon))$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon$, the explicit choice
$
n=\left\lceil16F_s^4s\,\varepsilon^{-4}[\log(1/\varepsilon)]^2\right\rceil
$
satisfies the required condition, because $\log n=4\log(1/\varepsilon)+2\log\log(1/\varepsilon)+\mathcal O(1)$ and hence
$
\frac{n}{(1+\log n)(16F_s^4s\,\varepsilon^{-4})}\rightarrow\infty.
$
Thus $n=\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\varepsilon^{-4})$ and $k=mn=\widetilde{\mathcal O}(\varepsilon^{-4})$.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Set $\tau=1/g_0$ and $u_x=\kappa_\tau(x,\cdot)-1$. Symmetry and the semigroup identity give, for $v\geq0$,
$$
\pi(u_x)=0,\qquad
\|u_x\|_2^2=\kappa_{2\tau}(x,x)-1,\qquad
\kappa_{2\tau+v}(x,y)-1=\langle u_x,P_vu_y\rangle_2.
$$
In particular, the kernel identities follow by expanding $u_x=\kappa_\tau(x,\cdot)-1$ and applying symmetry and the semigroup property:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\|u_x\|_2^2&=\int_{\mathcal C}\kappa_\tau(x,z)\kappa_\tau(z,x)\pi(dz)-1=\kappa_{2\tau}(x,x)-1,\\
\langle u_x,P_vu_y\rangle_2&=\int_{\mathcal C}\int_{\mathcal C}\kappa_\tau(x,z)\kappa_v(z,w)\kappa_\tau(w,y)\pi(dw)\pi(dz)-1=\kappa_{2\tau+v}(x,y)-1, \quad v>0.
\end{aligned}
$$
For $v=0$, the second identity follows directly from $P_0=I$. Since $\pi(u_y)=0$, the spectral gap gives $\|P_vu_y\|_2\leq e^{-g_0v}\|u_y\|_2$. Consequently,
$$
\left|\langle u_x,P_vu_y\rangle_2\right|\leq e^{-g_0v}\sqrt{\kappa_{2\tau}(x,x)-1}\sqrt{\kappa_{2\tau}(y,y)-1}\leq e^{-g_0v}A_{\rm hk}.
$$
Taking the supremum gives
$$
\sup_{x,y}|\kappa_{2\tau+v}(x,y)-1|
\leq \exp(-g_0v)A_{\rm hk}.
$$
At $v=s_*-2/g_0=g_0^{-1}\log\max\{1,2A_{\rm hk}\}$, the right side is at most $1/2$. Thus $m_{s_*}\geq1/2$ and $M_{s_*}\leq3/2$. Using $s_*=a_*/g_0$ in \eqref{eq:const:heatkernel:constants}, we obtain
$$
\begin{aligned}
H_{s_*} &=\exp(\ell_\sigma^2s_*)\sqrt{\frac{M_{s_*}}{2e\,g_0s_*m_{s_*}}}
\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\ell_\sigma^2}{g_0}a_*\right)\sqrt{\frac{3/2}{2e\,a_*(1/2)}}\\
&=\exp\!\left(\frac{\ell_\sigma^2}{g_0}a_*\right)\sqrt{\frac{3}{2e\,a_*}}
\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\ell_\sigma^2}{g_0}a_*\right)\sqrt{\frac{3}{2a_*}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $g_0s_*=a_*\geq2$ and $e^{\ell_\sigma^2s_*}\geq1$, the definitions in~\eqref{eq:const:Fs} and~\eqref{eq:const:Is} give
$$
\begin{aligned}
