B_{s_*}&\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s_*}\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{3/(2ea_*)}}{1-e^{-a_*}}\right)\leq e^{\ell_\sigma^2s_*}\left(2+\frac{\sqrt{3/(4e)}}{1-e^{-2}}\right)\leq3e^{\ell_\sigma^2s_*},\\
I_{s_*}&=\left(1+\sqrt{e^{4\ell_\sigma^2s_*}-1}\right)\sqrt{G_{s_*}}\leq2e^{2\ell_\sigma^2s_*}\sqrt{G_{s_*}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Under $\ell_\sigma^2/g_0\leq\ell_*$, their product satisfies
$$
F_{s_*}\leq6\exp\left(3\ell_\sigma^2s_*\right)\sqrt{G_{s_*}}
\leq6\exp(6\ell_*)\max\{1,2A_{\rm hk}\}^{3\ell_*}\sqrt{G_{s_*}},
$$
which proves \eqref{eq:const:conditioning:Fs}. Finally, $\operatorname{Lip}(\sigma)\leq\sqrt\Lambda\,\operatorname{Lip}(U)/2$ gives $\ell_\sigma^2\leq d\Lambda\,\operatorname{Lip}(U)^2/4$, proving the stated sufficient condition.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
For every $\mu>0$, $g_\mu$ is smooth, $g$ has at most linear growth, while every derivative of the Gaussian kernel is integrable against that growth, locally uniformly in $x$. Differentiation under the convolution integral is therefore valid. Since $g_\mu(x)=\mathbb E[g(x+\mu\xi)]$ and $\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,I_d)$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
    |h_\mu(x)|
    &=\left|g(x)-\mathbb E[g(x+\mu\xi)]\right|\\
    &\leq\mathbb E|g(x)-g(x+\mu\xi)|
    \leq K\mu\mathbb E\|\xi\|
    \leq K\mu\left(\mathbb E\|\xi\|^2\right)^{1/2}
    =K\mu\sqrt d.
\end{aligned}
$$
This proves \eqref{eq:Gaussian:hmu:sup}. For any $x,y\in\mathcal C$, the Lipschitz continuity of $g$ with constant $K$ gives the same Lipschitz bound for $g_\mu$,
\begin{align}
|g_{\mu}(x)-g_{\mu}(y)|
&=\left|\mathbb E\!\left[g(x+\mu\xi)-g(y+\mu\xi)\right]\right|\leq \mathbb E\!\left[
|g(x+\mu\xi)-g(y+\mu\xi)|
\right]\leq K\|x-y\|.
\end{align}
Hence,
\begin{align}
|h_{\mu}(x)-h_{\mu}(y)|
\leq |g(x)-g(y)|+|g_{\mu}(x)-g_{\mu}(y)|\leq 2K\|x-y\|,
\end{align}
which proves~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:hmu:Lipschitz}. Since $g_{\mu}$ is differentiable and Lipschitz with constant $K$, for every unit vector $v\in\mathbb R^d$,
\begin{equation}
|\langle\nabla g_{\mu}(x),v\rangle|
=\lim_{t\to0}
\frac{|g_{\mu}(x+tv)-g_{\mu}(x)|}{|t|}
\leq K.
\end{equation}
Taking the supremum over $\|v\|=1$ gives $\|\nabla g_{\mu}(x)\|\leq K$. Since $0\in\mathcal C\subset B(0,R)$,
\begin{align}
\|\nabla U_{\mu}(x)\|
&\leq \|\nabla f(x)\|+\|\nabla g_{\mu}(x)\|\nonumber\\
&\leq \|\nabla f(0)\|+L_f\|x\|+K
\leq \|\nabla f(0)\|+L_fR+K.
\end{align}
Taking the supremum over $x\in\mathcal C$ proves \eqref{eq:Gaussian:Gmu:explicit}.

It remains to verify the weak convexity and smoothness of $g_\mu$. Fix $x,y\in\mathbb R^d$ and $\lambda\in[0,1]$. Applying the weak convexity inequality with parameter $\rho$ in \eqref{assumption:g:convex:Gaussian:smoothing} to the two points $x+\mu\xi$ and $y+\mu\xi$, we obtain, for every $\xi\in\mathbb R^d$,
\begin{align}
g\!\left(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y+\mu\xi\right)
&=g\!\left(\lambda(x+\mu\xi)
+(1-\lambda)(y+\mu\xi)\right)\nonumber\\
&\leq \lambda g(x+\mu\xi)+(1-\lambda)g(y+\mu\xi)
+\frac{\rho}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)
\|(x+\mu\xi)-(y+\mu\xi)\|^2\nonumber\\
&=\lambda g(x+\mu\xi)+(1-\lambda)g(y+\mu\xi)
+\frac{\rho}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)\|x-y\|^2.
\end{align}
Taking expectations with respect to $\xi$ on both sides yields
\begin{align}
g_{\mu}\!\left(\lambda x+(1-\lambda)y\right)
&\leq \lambda g_{\mu}(x)+(1-\lambda)g_{\mu}(y)
+\frac{\rho}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)\|x-y\|^2.
\end{align}
This is precisely the weak convexity inequality with parameter $\rho$ in \eqref{assumption:g:convex:Gaussian:smoothing}. Hence $g_{\mu}$ is weakly convex with parameter $\rho$. Differentiating the Gaussian convolution kernel and using $\mathbb E\xi=0$ gives
$$
    \nabla g_\mu(x)
    =\frac1\mu\mathbb E\left[\xi g(x+\mu\xi)\right] =\frac1\mu\mathbb E\left[\xi\left(g(x+\mu\xi)-g(x)\right)\right].
$$
Consequently,
\begin{align}
\|\nabla g_{\mu}(x)-\nabla g_{\mu}(y)\|
&\leq \frac1\mu\mathbb E\!\left[
\|\xi\|\,|g(x+\mu\xi)-g(y+\mu\xi)|
\right]\leq \frac{K}{\mu}\mathbb E\|\xi\|\,\|x-y\|
\leq \frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}\|x-y\|.
\end{align}
Since $g_\mu$ is differentiable and weakly convex with parameter $\rho$, the function
$$
    x\mapsto g_\mu(x)+\frac{\rho}{2}\|x\|^2
$$
is differentiable and convex. The monotonicity of its gradient gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
0 &\leq
\left\langle
\nabla g_\mu(x)+\rho x-\nabla g_\mu(y)-\rho y,
x-y
\right\rangle=
\langle\nabla g_\mu(x)-\nabla g_\mu(y),x-y\rangle
+\rho\|x-y\|^2.
\end{aligned}
$$
Therefore,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\langle\nabla U_\mu(x)-\nabla U_\mu(y),x-y\rangle
&=
\langle\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y),x-y\rangle
+\langle\nabla g_\mu(x)-\nabla g_\mu(y),x-y\rangle\\
&\geq(m_f-\rho)\|x-y\|^2.
\end{aligned}
$$
The two gradient Lipschitz bounds also give
$$
\begin{aligned}
\|\nabla U_\mu(x)-\nabla U_\mu(y)\|
&\leq
\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|
+\|\nabla g_\mu(x)-\nabla g_\mu(y)\|\leq
\left(L_f+\frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}\right)\|x-y\|.
\end{aligned}
$$
The proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
Fix $x,y\in\mathcal C$ with $x\neq y$ and define
$$
    r_\mu(x) :=\nabla U_\mu(x)(e^{h_\mu(x)}-1).
$$
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gaussian:b:perturbation}
    b(x)=-\nabla U_\mu(x)-r_\mu(x).
\end{equation}
By the definition of $\varepsilon_{b,\mu}$,
$$
    \|r_\mu(x)-r_\mu(y)\|
    \leq
    \varepsilon_{b,\mu}\|x-y\|.
$$
Since $U_\mu$ is strongly convex with constant $m_f-\rho$,
\begin{align}
    \langle b(x)-b(y),x-y\rangle
    &=
    -\langle\nabla U_\mu(x)-\nabla U_\mu(y),x-y\rangle
    -\langle r_\mu(x)-r_\mu(y),x-y\rangle
    \nonumber\\
    &\leq
    -(m_f-\rho)\|x-y\|^2
    +\varepsilon_{b,\mu}\|x-y\|^2
    =
    -m_\mu\|x-y\|^2.
    \label{eq:Gaussian:b:dissipativity}
\end{align}
By Lemma~\ref{lemma:propositions of Gaussian smoothing},
$$
    \left\|\nabla U_\mu(x)-\nabla U_\mu(y)\right\|
    \leq
    \left(L_f+\frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}\right)\|x-y\|.
$$
Combining this estimate with \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:perturbation} gives
\begin{align}
    \|b(x)-b(y)\|
    &\leq
    \|\nabla U_\mu(x)-\nabla U_\mu(y)\|
    +\|r_\mu(x)-r_\mu(y)\|
    \nonumber\\
    &\leq
    \left(
    L_f+\frac{K\sqrt d}{\mu}
    +\varepsilon_{b,\mu}
    \right)\|x-y\|
   =M_\mu\|x-y\|.
    \label{eq:Gaussian:b:Lipschitz}
\end{align}
Moreover,
\begin{align}
    \left\|\sigma(x)I_d-\sigma(y)I_d\right\|_{\mathrm F}
    =
    \sqrt d\,
    \left|e^{h_\mu(x)/2}-e^{h_\mu(y)/2}\right|
    \leq
    \sqrt d\,\varepsilon_{\sigma,\mu}\|x-y\|
    =
    \sqrt{\alpha_\mu}\|x-y\|.
    \label{eq:Gaussian:sigma:Lipschitz}
\end{align}
It remains to verify the required relations among the constants. From \eqref{eq:Gaussian:perturbation:small},
$$
    \varepsilon_{b,\mu}
    +2\alpha_\mu < m_f-\rho-\frac12.
$$
Therefore
$$
\begin{aligned}
    m_\mu &=m_f-\rho-\varepsilon_{b,\mu}>\frac12+2\alpha_\mu >\alpha_\mu,
\end{aligned}
$$
so $m_\mu>0$ and $0\leq\alpha_\mu<m_\mu$. Hence \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:dissipativity}, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:Lipschitz}, and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:sigma:Lipschitz} verify Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:strongly:convex} and \ref{asmpt:smooth}. Finally,
$$
    2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu = 2\left( m_f-\rho-\frac12 -\varepsilon_{b,\mu} -2\alpha_\mu
    \right)
    = 2\left( m_f-\rho-\frac12 -\varepsilon_{b,\mu} -2d\varepsilon_{\sigma,\mu}^2
    \right)
    >0.
$$
Thus, $2m_\mu>1+4\alpha_\mu$, and all coefficient assumptions of Theorem~\ref{global:error:2} hold.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Throughout this proof, $\mathbb E$ denotes expectation with respect to all random variables in the synchronous coupling. For fixed $x\in\mathcal C$, let $\mathbb E_{p_N,k}$ denote expectation only over the $N$ samples $\{\xi_{i,k}\}_{i=1}^N$ used to approximate the Gaussian smoothing function at iteration $k$, and let $\mathbb E_{g_N,k}$ denote expectation only over the $N$ samples $\{\hat\xi_{i,k}\}_{i=1}^N$ used to approximate its gradient. All other quantities are regarded as constants under these two expectations.

Before projection, the difference between the two synchronously coupled algorithms is
\begin{align}
&\tilde x_k-x_k
+\eta\bigl(\tilde b_k(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k)\bigr)
+\sqrt{2\eta}\bigl(\tilde\sigma_k(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(x_k)\bigr)
\xi_{k+1}.
\end{align}
By the nonexpansiveness of the orthogonal projection onto the convex set $\mathcal C$, we obtain
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\|\tilde x_{k+1}-x_{k+1}\|^2
&\leq\mathbb E\Bigl\|\tilde x_k-x_k
+\eta\bigl(\tilde b_k(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k)\bigr)
+\sqrt{2\eta}\bigl(\tilde\sigma_k(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(x_k)\bigr)
\xi_{k+1}\Bigr\|^2
\nonumber\\
&=\mathbb E\Bigl\|\tilde x_k-x_k
+\eta\bigl(b(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k)\bigr)
+\eta\bigl(\tilde b_k(\tilde x_k)-b(\tilde x_k)\bigr)\Bigr\|^2
\nonumber\\
&\quad+2\eta d\,\mathbb E\left[\Bigl|
\sigma(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(x_k)
+\tilde\sigma_k(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(\tilde x_k)\Bigr|^2\right]\nonumber\\
&\leq(1+\eta)\mathbb E
\left[\|\tilde x_k-x_k+\eta(b(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k))\|^2\right]
+\eta(1+\eta)
\mathbb E\left[\left\|\tilde b_k(\tilde x_k)-b(\tilde x_k)\right\|^2\right]\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad+4\eta d\,\mathbb E\left[\left|\sigma(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(x_k)\right|^2\right]
+4\eta d\,\mathbb E\left[|\tilde\sigma_k(\tilde x_k)
-\sigma(\tilde x_k)|^2\right],
\label{eq:Gaussian:coupling:first}
\end{align}
where the equality uses that $\xi_{k+1}$ is independent of all quantities at iteration $k$, $\mathbb E[\xi_{k+1}]=0$, and $\mathbb E\|\xi_{k+1}\|^2=d$. The last inequality is obtained by applying $2\langle a,b\rangle\leq\eta\|a\|^2+\eta^{-1}\|b\|^2$ to the first term and applying $|a+b|^2\leq2|a|^2+2|b|^2$ to the second term.

The first term in \eqref{eq:Gaussian:coupling:first} is controlled by the dissipativity and Lipschitz bounds for the ideal drift $b$ in \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:dissipativity} and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:Lipschitz}:
\begin{align}
\|\tilde x_k-x_k+\eta(b(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k))\|^2&=\|\tilde x_k-x_k\|^2
+2\eta\langle\tilde x_k-x_k,b(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k)\rangle
+\eta^2\|b(\tilde x_k)-b(x_k)\|^2\nonumber\\
&\leq(1-2m_\mu\eta+M_\mu^2\eta^2)
\|\tilde x_k-x_k\|^2.
\label{eq:Gaussian:ideal:Euler:contraction}
\end{align}
The third term is controlled directly by the coefficient condition for $\sigma$:
\begin{equation}
d|\sigma(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(x_k)|^2
=\|\sigma(\tilde x_k)I_d-\sigma(x_k)I_d\|_{\mathrm F}^2
\leq\alpha_\mu\|\tilde x_k-x_k\|^2.
\label{eq:Gaussian:ideal:sigma:difference}
\end{equation}
The remaining terms are required to bound the approximation errors from the Monte Carlo simulation. For any fixed $x\in\mathcal C$, decompose the drift and diffusion errors as
\begin{align}
\tilde b_k(x)-b(x)
&=-\bigl(\widetilde{\nabla} U_{\mu,k}(x)-\nabla U_\mu(x)\bigr)
e^{h_\mu(x)-\left(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x)\right)}\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\nabla U_\mu(x)e^{h_\mu(x)}
\left(e^{-\left(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x)\right)}-1\right),
\label{eq:Gaussian:b:MC:difference}\\
\tilde\sigma_k(x)-\sigma(x)
&=e^{h_\mu(x)/2}
\left(e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))/2}-1\right).
\label{eq:Gaussian:sigma:MC:difference}
\end{align}
Thus, the first summand in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:MC:difference} requires a second moment bound for the gradient estimator and an exponential moment bound for the function estimator. The second summand in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:MC:difference} and the error in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:sigma:MC:difference} require only exponential moment bounds for the function estimator. We derive these bounds in that order. For the gradient estimator, the Gaussian integration by parts identity and $\mathbb E_{g_N,k}[\hat\xi_{i,k}]=0$ give
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{g_N,k}\left[\widetilde{\nabla} U_{\mu,k}(x)\right]
&=\nabla f(x)+\frac{1}{\mu N}\sum_{i=1}^N
\mathbb E_{g_N,k}\left[
\hat\xi_{i,k}\left(g\left(x+\mu\hat\xi_{i,k}\right)-g(x)\right)\right]\nonumber\\
&=\nabla f(x)+\nabla g_\mu(x)=\nabla U_\mu(x).
\end{align}
Independence within the simulation therefore gives
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{g_N,k}\left\|\widetilde{\nabla} U_{\mu,k}(x)-\nabla U_\mu(x)\right\|^2
&\leq\frac1N\mathbb E_{g_N,k}\left\|
\frac{\hat\xi_{1,k}}{\mu}
\left(g\left(x+\mu\hat\xi_{1,k}\right)-g(x)\right)\right\|^2\nonumber\\
&\leq\frac{K^2}{N}\mathbb E_{g_N,k}\|\hat\xi_{1,k}\|^4
=\frac{K^2d(d+2)}{N}.
\label{eq:Gaussian:gradient:MC:bound}
\end{align}
Here the variance of the average of $N$ independent centered vectors is $1/N$ times the variance of one vector. The Lipschitz bound $|g(x+\mu\hat\xi)-g(x)|\leq K\mu\|\hat\xi\|$ and the Gaussian moment $\mathbb E\|\hat\xi\|^4=d(d+2)$ give the remaining steps. For the function estimator, define
$$
    F_x(z_1,\ldots,z_N):=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N g(x+\mu z_i).
$$
For $z=(z_1,\ldots,z_N)$ and $z'=(z_1',\ldots,z_N')$ in $\mathbb R^{dN}$,
$$
    |F_x(z)-F_x(z')|
    \leq\frac{K\mu}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N\|z_i-z_i'\|
    \leq\frac{K\mu}{\sqrt N}
    \left[\sum_{i=1}^N\|z_i-z_i'\|^2\right]^{1/2}.
$$
Thus, $F_x$ is $K\mu/\sqrt N$ Lipschitz on $\mathbb R^{dN}$. Since $(\xi_{1,k},\ldots,\xi_{N,k})$ is standard Gaussian in $\mathbb R^{dN}$ and
$$
    \tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x)
    =F_x(\xi_{1,k},\ldots,\xi_{N,k}) -\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[F_x(\xi_{1,k},\ldots,\xi_{N,k})\right],
$$
the Gaussian concentration inequality gives
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[\exp\!\left(\lambda
\bigl(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x)\bigr)\right)\right]
\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\lambda^2K^2\mu^2}{2N}\right),
\qquad \forall\lambda\in\mathbb R.
\label{eq:Gaussian:potential:mgf}
\end{equation}
Unbiasedness of the Monte Carlo simulation and Jensen's inequality give, for every $a>0$,
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[
e^{-a(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}\right]\geq1.
\label{eq:Gaussian:potential:Jensen}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:mgf} with $\lambda=-2$ and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:Jensen} gives
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left |e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}-1\right |^2
&=\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[e^{-2(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}\right]
-2\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}\right]+1\nonumber\\
&\leq e^{2K^2\mu^2/N}-1,
\label{eq:Gaussian:potential:exp:error}
\end{align}
Returning to the expectation of the drift error \eqref{eq:Gaussian:b:MC:difference}, the unbiasedness of the Monte Carlo gradient estimator makes the cross term vanish after taking $\mathbb E_{g_N,k}$. Taking $\mathbb E_{p_N,k}$ then gives
\begin{align}
& \mathbb E_{p_N,k}\mathbb E_{g_N,k}
\left\|\tilde b_k(x)-b(x)\right\|^2
\nonumber\\
&\qquad=e^{2h_\mu(x)}
\mathbb E_{g_N,k}
\left\|\widetilde{\nabla} U_{\mu,k}(x)-\nabla U_\mu(x)\right\|^2
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[e^{-2(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}\right]\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left\|\nabla U_\mu(x)\right\|^2e^{2h_\mu(x)}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}
\left[\left(e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}-1\right)^2\right]
\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq \frac{K^2d(d+2)}{N}
e^{2K\mu\sqrt d+2K^2\mu^2/N}\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(\|\nabla f(0)\|+L_fR+K\right)^2
e^{2K\mu\sqrt d}\left(e^{2K^2\mu^2/N}-1\right)
=:A_{\mu,N}.
\end{align}
Here in the inequality, the first term uses \eqref{eq:Gaussian:hmu:sup}, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:gradient:MC:bound} and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:mgf} with $\lambda=-2$. The second term uses \eqref{eq:Gaussian:Gmu:explicit}, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:exp:error}, and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:hmu:sup}. For the diffusion error in \eqref{eq:Gaussian:sigma:MC:difference}, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:mgf} with $\lambda=-1$ and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:potential:Jensen} with $a=1/2$ give
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}
\left[\left(e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))/2}-1\right)^2\right]
&=\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[ e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))}\right] -2\mathbb E_{p_N,k}
\left[e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))/2}\right]+1\\
&\leq e^{K^2\mu^2/(2N)}-1.
\end{aligned}
$$
Combining this estimate with \eqref{eq:Gaussian:hmu:sup} yields
\begin{align}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\left[|\tilde\sigma_k(x)-\sigma(x)|^2\right]
&=e^{h_\mu(x)}
\mathbb E_{p_N,k}
\left[\left(e^{-(\tilde U_{\mu,k}(x)-U_\mu(x))/2}-1\right)^2\right]
\nonumber\\
&\leq e^{K\mu\sqrt d}
\left(e^{K^2\mu^2/(2N)}-1\right)
=B_{\mu,N}.
\end{align}
Since $\tilde x_k$ is independent of all the Monte Carlo samples at iteration $k$, iterated expectation gives
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\left\|\tilde b_k(\tilde x_k)-b(\tilde x_k)\right\|^2
&=\mathbb E\left[
\left.\mathbb E_{p_N,k}\mathbb E_{g_N,k}
\|\tilde b_k(x)-b(x)\|^2\right |{x=\tilde x_k}\right]
\leq A_{\mu,N},\nonumber\\
\mathbb E\left|\tilde\sigma_k(\tilde x_k)-\sigma(\tilde x_k)\right|^2
&=\mathbb E\left[
\left.\mathbb E_{p_N,k}|\tilde\sigma_k(x)-\sigma(x)|^2
\right |{x=\tilde x_k}\right]
\leq B_{\mu,N}.
\label{eq:Gaussian:MC:random:bounds}
\end{align}
Substituting~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:ideal:Euler:contraction}, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:ideal:sigma:difference}, and \eqref{eq:Gaussian:MC:random:bounds} into \eqref{eq:Gaussian:coupling:first} closes the one step update:
\begin{align}
\mathbb E\|\tilde x_{k+1}-x_{k+1}\|^2
&\leq\left[(1+\eta)\left(1-2m_\mu\eta+M_\mu^2\eta^2\right)
+4\alpha_\mu\eta\right]\mathbb E\|\tilde x_k-x_k\|^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\qquad+\eta\left((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\right).
\label{eq:Gaussian:coupling:recursion}
\end{align}

We finally solve the resulting recursion. Under \eqref{eq:Gaussian:eta:conservative}, the multiplicative coefficient above satisfies
\begin{align}
&(1+\eta)(1-2m_\mu\eta+M_\mu^2\eta^2)
+4\alpha_\mu\eta
\nonumber
\\
&\qquad=1-\left(2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu\right)\eta
+(M_\mu^2-2m_\mu)\eta^2+M_\mu^2\eta^3\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq1-(2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu)\eta
+\left(\max\left\{0,M_\mu^2-2m_\mu\right\}+M_\mu^2\right)\eta^2\nonumber\\
&\qquad\leq1-\frac{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}{2}\eta.
\end{align}
Since $m_\mu\leq M_\mu$,
\begin{equation}
1-2m_\mu\eta+M_\mu^2\eta^2
=(1-m_\mu\eta)^2+(M_\mu^2-m_\mu^2)\eta^2\geq0.
\end{equation}
We have $1-(2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu)\eta/2$ lies in $[0,1)$. Starting from $\tilde x_0=x_0$, iterate~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:coupling:recursion} to obtain
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\|\tilde x_k-x_k\|^2
&\leq\eta\bigl((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\bigr)\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}{2}\eta\right)^j\\
&=\frac{2\bigl((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\bigr)}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}{2}\eta\right)^k\right]\\
&\leq\frac{2\bigl((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\bigr)}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}.
\end{aligned}
$$
For $k=0$, the same final bound holds because the error is zero. Finally, the synchronous construction above is itself a coupling of $\nu_k=\mathrm{Law}(x_k)$ and $\tilde\nu_k=\mathrm{Law}(\tilde x_k)$. Hence,
$$
\mathcal W_2^2(\nu_k,\tilde\nu_k)
\leq
\mathbb E\|x_k-\tilde x_k\|^2
\leq
\frac{2}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}
\left((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\right),
$$
which is exactly \eqref{eq:Gaussian:MC:W2}. The proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
Fix $\mu$ satisfying the hypotheses. By~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:m:M:alpha} and~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:perturbation:small},
$$
2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu=2\left(m_f-\rho-\frac12-\varepsilon_{b,\mu}-2d\varepsilon_{\sigma,\mu}^2\right)>0.
$$
Lemma~\ref{lemma:Gaussian:sufficient:conditions} therefore verifies Assumptions~\ref{asmpt:strongly:convex} and~\ref{asmpt:smooth} for the exact coefficients in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:exact:coefficients}, with $(m,M,\alpha)=(m_\mu,M_\mu,\alpha_\mu)$. The present theorem supplies the domain regularity, potential regularity, Poincar\'e inequality, and initial law assumptions required for continuous time convergence. In particular, Proposition~\ref{prop:continuous:structure} supplies the transition semigroup and heat kernel properties. The common initial law also satisfies the interior support condition in Theorem~\ref{thm:final}. Finally, \eqref{eq:Gaussian:eta:conservative} implies $0<\eta\leq1$, and $k\eta\geq e$ is assumed. Thus Theorem~\ref{thm:final} applies to the ideal iterates $x_k$.

Now substituting $U_0=U_\mu$ and the constants in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:Cbar} into~\eqref{eq:thm:final:explicit} gives
$$
\mathcal W_2(\nu_k,\pi)\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\nu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha_{0,\mu}k\eta/(2C_P)}+\bar C_{0,\mu}\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2}.
$$
Here the first term is the continuous time mixing error and the second is the discretization error. For the same initial law and stepsize, Lemma~\ref{lemma:Gaussian:MC:error} applies to the ideal and Monte Carlo iterates. Taking square roots in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:MC:W2} yields
$$
\mathcal W_2(\tilde\nu_k,\nu_k)\leq\left(\frac{2\bigl((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\bigr)}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}\right)^{1/2}.
$$
Consequently, the triangle inequality gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2(\tilde\nu_k,\pi)&\leq\mathcal W_2(\tilde\nu_k,\nu_k)+\mathcal W_2(\nu_k,\pi)\\
&\leq 2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\nu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha_{0,\mu}k\eta/(2C_P)}+\bar C_{0,\mu}\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2}\\
&\qquad+\left(\frac{2\bigl((1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\bigr)}{2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu}\right)^{1/2},
\end{aligned}
$$
which proves~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:final:W2}.

Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and keep $\mu$ fixed. Since $\alpha_{0,\mu}\geq e^{-K\mu\sqrt d}>0$ by~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:Cbar} and the initial $\chi^2$ divergence is finite, choose a finite $T\geq e$ such that
$$
2^{3/4}R\left(\chi^2(\nu_0\Vert\pi)\right)^{1/4}e^{-\alpha_{0,\mu}T/(2C_P)}\leq\frac\varepsilon3.
$$
For each integer $k$, set $\eta=T/k$. With $T$ fixed, the discretization term becomes
\begin{align}\label{discretization:term}
\bar C_{0,\mu}\eta^{1/4}(k\eta)^{1/2}(\log k)^{1/2}=\bar C_{0,\mu}T^{3/4}\left(\frac{(\log k)^2}{k}\right)^{1/4}\rightarrow0.
\end{align}
The upper bound on $\eta$ in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:eta:conservative} is strictly positive and independent of $k$ and $N$. Since $T/k\to0$, one can choose an integer $k$ large enough that this stepsize condition holds and the discretization term in \eqref{discretization:term} is at most $\varepsilon/3$. This choice preserves $k\eta=T\geq e$.

It remains to choose $N$ with $T,k,\eta$ now fixed. To verify the required decay directly from~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:A} and~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:B}, use $e^t-1=\int_0^t e^s\,ds\leq te^t$ for $t\geq0$. Since $N\geq1$, this gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
&A_{\mu,N}\leq\frac{e^{2K\mu\sqrt d+2K^2\mu^2}}{N}\left[K^2d(d+2)+2K^2\mu^2\left(\|\nabla f(0)\|+L_fR+K\right)^2\right],
\\
&B_{\mu,N}\leq\frac{K^2\mu^2}{2N}e^{K\mu\sqrt d+K^2\mu^2/2}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus both quantities are $\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})$ for the fixed model and smoothing level, and the Monte Carlo error is $\mathcal{O}(N^{-1/2})$. Choose an integer $N$ large enough that
$$
(1+\eta)A_{\mu,N}+4dB_{\mu,N}\leq\frac{\varepsilon^2}{18}\left(2m_\mu-1-4\alpha_\mu\right).
$$
The square root term in~\eqref{eq:Gaussian:final:W2} is then at most $\varepsilon/3$. Each of the three errors is at most $\varepsilon/3$, so their sum is at most $\varepsilon$, the proof is complete.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
We first bound the normalized scaling factor, then the derivatives of the drift and diffusion coefficients in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:coefficients}. Since $g_\delta$ is convex and $K$-Lipschitz, Jensen's inequality and $\mathbb E|G|\leq1$ give
$$
-K\mu\leq U_\delta-U_{\delta,\mu}\leq0,
\qquad
e^{-K\mu}\leq a_\delta^{\rm n}(x) =\frac{e^{U_\delta(x)-U_{\delta,\mu}(x)}}{\int_{\mathcal C}e^{U_\delta-U_{\delta,\mu}}\,d\pi_\delta}
\leq e^{K\mu}.
$$
The quadratic parts cancel in $U_\delta'-U_{\delta,\mu}'$, so
$$
|U_{\delta,\mu}'|\leq\kappa R+K,\qquad |U_\delta'-U_{\delta,\mu}'|\leq2K,\qquad
\kappa\leq U_{\delta,\mu}''\leq\kappa+K/\mu.
$$
The last bound follows from convexity and Gaussian integration by parts:
$U_{\delta,\mu}''-\kappa=\mu^{-1}\mathbb E[Gg_\delta'(x+\mu G)]$, with $|g_\delta'|\leq K$.

For the drift, differentiating $b_\delta^{\rm n}=-a_\delta^{\rm n}U_{\delta,\mu}'$ gives
$$
(b_\delta^{\rm n})' =-a_\delta^{\rm n}\bigl[U_{\delta,\mu}'' +(U_\delta'-U_{\delta,\mu}')U_{\delta,\mu}'\bigr].
$$
Since~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility} implies $\kappa-2K(\kappa R+K)>0$, the preceding bounds yield
\begin{align*}
(b_\delta^{\rm n})'\leq-e^{-K\mu}\bigl[\kappa-2K(\kappa R+K)\bigr],\qquad
|(b_\delta^{\rm n})'|\leq e^{K\mu}\bigl[\kappa+K/\mu+2K(\kappa R+K)\bigr].
\end{align*}
For the diffusion, differentiating $\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}=\sqrt{a_\delta^{\rm n}}$ gives directly
$$
|(\sigma_\delta^{\rm n})'|^2 =\tfrac14a_\delta^{\rm n}|U_\delta'-U_{\delta,\mu}'|^2
\leq e^{K\mu}K^2.
$$
The fundamental theorem of calculus gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
&(b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y))(x-y)=(x-y)^2\int_0^1(b_\delta^{\rm n})'(y+t(x-y))\,dt\leq-e^{-K\mu}[\kappa-2K(\kappa R+K)]|x-y|^2,\\
&|b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|\leq|x-y|\int_0^1|(b_\delta^{\rm n})'(y+t(x-y))|\,dt\leq e^{K\mu}[\kappa+K/\mu+2K(\kappa R+K)]|x-y|.
\end{aligned}
$$
Similarly, Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on $[0,1]$ gives
$$
|\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|^2\leq|x-y|^2\int_0^1|(\sigma_\delta^{\rm n})'(y+t(x-y))|^2\,dt\leq e^{K\mu}K^2|x-y|^2.
$$
These are exactly the three estimates in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:bounds}.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
Fix $\delta>0$ and $0<\eta<\eta_c$. For deterministic $x,y\in\mathcal C$, use the same $G\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ to define
$$
\begin{aligned}
X_1^x&=\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}\!\left(x+\eta b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)
+\sqrt{2\eta}\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(x)G\right),\quad
X_1^y=\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}\!\left(y+\eta b_\delta^{\rm n}(y) +\sqrt{2\eta}\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(y)G\right).
\end{aligned}
$$
These variables have marginal laws $Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}(x,\cdot)$ and $Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}(y,\cdot)$. Since projection onto $\mathcal C$ is nonexpansive, $\mathbb EG=0$, and $\mathbb EG^2=1$,
\begin{align*}
\mathbb E|X_1^x-X_1^y|^2
&\leq\mathbb E\left|x-y+\eta\bigl(b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y)\bigr)
+\sqrt{2\eta}\bigl(\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(y)\bigr)G\right|^2\\
&=|x-y|^2+2\eta(x-y)\bigl(b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y)\bigr)\\
&\qquad+\eta^2|b_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-b_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|^2
+2\eta|\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(x)-\sigma_\delta^{\rm n}(y)|^2.
\end{align*}
The mixed term involving $G$ vanishes because the other factors are deterministic. Substituting the three bounds in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:bounds}, in the same order, gives
\begin{align*}
\mathbb E|X_1^x-X_1^y|^2
&\leq\Bigl\{1-2\eta e^{-K\mu}\bigl[\kappa-2K(\kappa R+K)\bigr]
+\eta^2M_*^2+2\eta e^{K\mu}K^2\Bigr\}|x-y|^2\\
&=\bigl(1-2\gamma_*\eta+M_*^2\eta^2\bigr)|x-y|^2
=q_\eta^2|x-y|^2,
\end{align*}
where the equality uses the definition of $\gamma_*$ in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:constants}. That definition and~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:admissibility} imply $0<\gamma_*<e^{-K\mu}\kappa<M_*$. Therefore
$$
q_\eta^2 =(1-\gamma_*\eta)^2+(M_*^2-\gamma_*^2)\eta^2>0,
\qquad
q_\eta^2=1-\eta(2\gamma_*-M_*^2\eta)<1,
$$
the last inequality following from $0<\eta<2\gamma_*/M_*^2$.

Now let $(X_0,Y_0)$ be an optimal coupling of $\nu$ and $\nu'$, so that $\mathbb E|X_0-Y_0|^2=\mathcal W_2^2(\nu,\nu')$. Take $G$ independent of this pair and apply the two updates above with $x=X_0$ and $y=Y_0$. The resulting $(X_1,Y_1)$ is a coupling of $\nu Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$ and $\nu'Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$. Conditioning on $(X_0,Y_0)$ and then taking expectation yields
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal W_2^2\left(\nu Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta},\nu'Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}\right)
&\leq\mathbb E|X_1-Y_1|^2\leq q_\eta^2\mathbb E|X_0-Y_0|^2 =q_\eta^2\mathcal W_2^2(\nu,\nu').
\end{aligned}
$$
Taking square roots proves~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:contraction}.

Finally, $\mathcal P_2(\mathcal C)$ is complete in $\mathcal W_2$, and compactness of $\mathcal C$ ensures that every probability measure on $\mathcal C$ has finite second moment. The map $\nu\mapsto\nu Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$ sends this space into itself and is a strict contraction with factor $q_\eta<1$. The Banach fixed point theorem therefore gives a unique fixed point $\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$, that is,
$\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}Q^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}=\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$.
Using this invariance and writing $\nu_{k+1}=\nu_kQ^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}$, the one step estimate gives
$$
\mathcal W_2\left(\nu_{k+1},\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}\right)
\leq q_\eta\mathcal W_2\left(\nu_k,\pi^{\rm R}_{\delta,\eta}\right).
$$
Induction proves the stated $k$-step bound. Since $\gamma_*$ and $M_*$ depend only on $\kappa,K,R,\mu$, both $\eta_c$ and $q_\eta$ are independent of $\delta$.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
<!-- proof-48: body_md -->
The RALMC assertion follows from~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:contraction}, since the first condition in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:separation:condition} places $\eta$ in the interval~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:eta}. We prove the PLMC assertions by computing the response of the projected transition mean to a change in the initial point.

Fix $\delta,\eta>0$ and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:projected:mean}
F_\delta(x):=x-\eta U_\delta'(x),\qquad
m_\delta(x):=\int_{\mathcal C}z\,Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(x,dz)
=\mathbb E\left[\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}\left(F_\delta(x)+\sqrt{2\eta}G\right)\right].
\end{equation}
The last equality follows from~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:kernels}. For the potential in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:family},
$$
U_\delta'(x)=\kappa x+\frac{Kx}{\sqrt{x^2+\delta^2}},\qquad U_\delta''(x)=\kappa+\frac{K\delta^2}{(x^2+\delta^2)^{3/2}}.
$$
Hence $F_\delta(0)=0$ and $F_\delta'(0)=1-\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)$. Projection onto $[-R,R]$ is the clipping map $\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(z)=\min\{R,\max\{-R,z\}\}$, whose derivative is $\mathbf1_{(-R,R)}(z)$ away from $z=\pm R$. Since $\sqrt{2\eta}G$ has a continuous density, these two exceptional points have probability zero. Thus, almost surely,
$$
\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(F_\delta(x)+\sqrt{2\eta}G)
-\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(\sqrt{2\eta}G)}{x} =F_\delta'(0)\mathbf1_{\{|\sqrt{2\eta}G|<R\}}.
$$
To pass this limit through the expectation in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:projected:mean}, choose $0<\varepsilon<R$. Nonexpansiveness of projection and the mean value theorem bound the absolute difference quotient by
$$
\frac{|F_\delta(x)-F_\delta(0)|}{|x|}
\leq\sup_{|u|\leq\varepsilon}\left|F_\delta'(u)\right|<\infty,
\qquad 0<|x|\leq\varepsilon.
$$
This deterministic bound is integrable for the fixed $\delta,\eta$. Dominated convergence therefore yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:mean:derivative}
m_\delta'(0)=F_\delta'(0)\mathbb P\left(\left|\sqrt{2\eta}G\right|<R\right)
=\left[1-\eta\left(\kappa+\frac K\delta\right)\right]p_\eta.
\end{equation}

Then for any coupling $(Y,Z)$ of two probability measures $\rho,\rho'$ with finite second moments, Jensen's inequality gives $|\mathbb EY-\mathbb EZ|^2\leq\mathbb E|Y-Z|^2$. Taking the infimum over couplings yields
$$
\mathcal W_2(\rho,\rho')\geq\left|\int z\,\rho(dz)-\int z\,\rho'(dz)\right|.
$$
Apply this inequality to $Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(x,\cdot)$ and $Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(0,\cdot)$, whose means are defined in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:projected:mean}. Dividing by $|x|$ and using~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:mean:derivative} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GS:sharp:mean:W2:lower}
\liminf_{\substack{x\to0\\x\neq0}}
\frac{\mathcal W_2(Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(x,\cdot),Q^{\rm P}_{\delta,\eta}(0,\cdot))}{|x|}
\geq\lim_{x\to0}\frac{|m_\delta(x)-m_\delta(0)|}{|x|}=|1-\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)|p_\eta.
\end{equation}
Under~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:separation:condition}, $\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)>1+p_\eta^{-1}>1$. The absolute value in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:mean:W2:lower} is consequently $\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)-1$, and its product with $p_\eta$ is strictly greater than one. This proves~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:PL:expansion}.

Fix any $\eta\in(0,\eta_c)$, with $\eta_c$ given by~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:eta}. Since $R,\eta>0$, one has $p_\eta>0$, so the upper bound in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:delta:range} is positive. For every $\delta$ in that range,
$$
\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)>\eta K/\delta>1+p_\eta^{-1}.
$$
Thus both conditions in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:separation:condition} hold. Finally, if PLMC is globally nonexpansive, then the numerator in~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:mean:W2:lower} is at most $|x|$ for every $x\in\mathcal C$. Therefore, 
$
|1-\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)|p_\eta\leq1$ gives $
\eta(\kappa+K/\delta)\leq1+p_\eta^{-1}$. For $0<\eta\leq\eta_{\max}$,
$$
p_\eta=\mathbb P\!\left(|G|<\frac R{\sqrt{2\eta}}\right)
\geq\mathbb P\!\left(|G|<\frac R{\sqrt{2\eta_{\max}}}\right)
=p_{\eta_{\max}}>0.
$$
Combining these inequalities proves~\eqref{eq:GS:sharp:PL:necessary}. In particular,
$$
\eta\leq\frac{1+p_{\eta_{\max}}^{-1}}{\kappa+K/\delta}
\leq\frac{1+p_{\eta_{\max}}^{-1}}{K}\,\delta,
$$
which establishes the claimed $O(\delta)$ restriction with a constant independent of $\delta$.
<!-- end proof-48 -->
<!-- proof-49: body_md -->
Both Rayleigh quotients are taken over the same functions $f\in H^1(\mathcal C)$ with $\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)>0$. Since $\|\nabla f\|^2\geq0$, integrating the bounds $a_{{\rm n},\min}\leq a_{\rm n}(x)\leq a_{{\rm n},\max}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gap:energy:comparison}
a_{{\rm n},\min}\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f)
\leq\mathcal E_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}(f)
\leq a_{{\rm n},\max}\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f).
\end{equation}
For the lower bound, divide the left inequality in~\eqref{eq:gap:energy:comparison} by $\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)$ to obtain
$$
\frac{\mathcal E_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}(f)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)}
\geq a_{{\rm n},\min}
\frac{\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f)}
\geq a_{{\rm n},\min}\lambda_{\rm PLD}.
$$
Taking the infimum over all admissible $f$ yields
$\lambda_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}\geq a_{{\rm n},\min}\lambda_{\rm PLD}$.

For the upper bound, fix $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of $\lambda_{\rm PLD}$, there exists $f_\varepsilon\in H^1(\mathcal C)$ with $\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f_\varepsilon)>0$ such that
$$
\frac{\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f_\varepsilon)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f_\varepsilon)}
\leq\lambda_{\rm PLD}+\varepsilon.
$$
Using $f_\varepsilon$ as a test function for the RALD Rayleigh quotient and applying the right inequality in~\eqref{eq:gap:energy:comparison}, we obtain
$$
\lambda_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}
\leq\frac{\mathcal E_{\rm RALD}^{\rm n}(f_\varepsilon)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f_\varepsilon)}\leq a_{{\rm n},\max}
\frac{\mathcal E_{\rm PLD}(f_\varepsilon)}{\operatorname{Var}_\pi(f_\varepsilon)}
\leq a_{{\rm n},\max}(\lambda_{\rm PLD}+\varepsilon).
$$
Letting $\varepsilon\rightarrow0$ proves the upper bound in~\eqref{eq:gap:comparison}. This argument does not require either infimum to be attained.
<!-- end proof-49 -->
<!-- proof-50: body_md -->
If $u\notin\mathcal C$, then $\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(u)\in\partial\mathcal C$. Since $D_+\subset\operatorname{int}(\mathcal C)$, it follows that
$$
\begin{aligned}
    \left\{\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(Z_{\rm R}(x))\in D_+\right\}
    =\left\{Z_{\rm R}(x)\in D_+\right\},\quad
    \left\{\mathcal P_{\mathcal C}(Z_{\rm P}(x))\in D_+\right\}
    =\left\{Z_{\rm P}(x)\in D_+\right\}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus the projected crossing probabilities are integrals of the Gaussian proposal densities over $D_+$. For fixed $x\in B$, these densities are
$$
\begin{aligned}
p_{\rm R}(x,y)&=(4\pi\eta a_{\rm n}(x))^{-d/2}
\exp\left[-\frac{\|y-x+\eta a_{\rm n}(x)\nabla U_0(x)\|^2}{4\eta a_{\rm n}(x)}\right],\\
p_{\rm P}(x,y)&=(4\pi\eta)^{-d/2}
\exp\left[-\frac{\|y-x+\eta\nabla U(x)\|^2}{4\eta}\right].
\end{aligned}
$$
Expanding the squared norms, the quadratic, cross, and drift terms give, respectively,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof:bottleneck:density:ratio}
\begin{aligned}
\log\frac{p_{\rm R}(x,y)}{p_{\rm P}(x,y)}
&=-\frac d2\log a_{\rm n}(x)
+\frac{1-a_{\rm n}(x)^{-1}}{4\eta}\|y-x\|^2\\
&\qquad+\frac12\langle y-x,\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\rangle
+\frac\eta4\left(\|\nabla U(x)\|^2-a_{\rm n}(x)\|\nabla U_0(x)\|^2\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For $y\in D_+$, $r_x\leq\|y-x\|\leq\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)$. Since $a_{\rm n}(x)>1$, the quadratic term is bounded below using $r_x$. Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives
$$
\langle y-x,\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\rangle
\geq-\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)\|\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\|.
$$
Dropping the nonnegative term $\eta\|\nabla U(x)\|^2/4$ in~\eqref{eq:proof:bottleneck:density:ratio} and exponentiating gives, for every $y\in D_+$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
p_{\rm R}(x,y)&\geq a_{\rm n}(x)^{-d/2}
\exp\Bigg\{\frac{r_x^2}{4\eta}\left(1-\frac1{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right)-\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)}2\|\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\| -\frac\eta4a_{\rm n}(x)\|\nabla U_0(x)\|^2\Bigg\}
\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot
p_{\rm P}(x,y).
\end{aligned}
$$
The factor multiplying $p_{\rm P}(x,y)$ is independent of $y$. Integrating this inequality over $D_+$ therefore yields
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)
=\int_{D_+}p_{\rm R}(x,y)\,dy\\
&\,\geq a_{\rm n}(x)^{-d/2}
\exp\Bigg\{\frac{r_x^2}{4\eta}\left(1-\frac1{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right)-\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)}2\|\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\| -\frac\eta4a_{\rm n}(x)\|\nabla U_0(x)\|^2\Bigg\}
\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot
\int_{D_+}p_{\rm P}(x,y)\,dy.
\end{aligned}
$$
Since the Gaussian density is strictly positive and $|D_+|>0$,
$$
\int_{D_+}p_{\rm P}(x,y)\,dy
=\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)>0.
$$
Dividing the preceding inequality by this positive quantity cancels the integral on its right hand side and gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\frac{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm RALMC}(x,D_+)}{\mathbb P_\eta^{\rm PLMC}(x,D_+)}
\\
&\geq a_{\rm n}(x)^{-d/2}
\exp\Bigg\{\frac{r_x^2}{4\eta}\left(1-\frac1{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right)-\frac{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal C)}2\|\nabla U(x)-\nabla U_0(x)\| -\frac\eta4a_{\rm n}(x)\|\nabla U_0(x)\|^2\Bigg\},
\end{aligned}
$$
which is~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:pointwise:ratio}. For the uniform estimate, $a_{\rm n}(x)\leq A$ gives $a_{\rm n}(x)^{-d/2}\geq A^{-d/2}$. Also, $a_{\rm n}(x)\geq\rho>1$ and $r_x\geq r$ imply
$$
r_x^2\left(1-\frac1{a_{\rm n}(x)}\right)
\geq r^2\left(1-\frac1\rho\right).
$$
Finally, bound each of the two nonnegative drift penalties by its supremum over $B$. Substitution into~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:pointwise:ratio} gives~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:finite:ratio}.
<!-- end proof-50 -->
<!-- proof-51: body_md -->
We first compute the logarithmic crossing rate for a Gaussian proposal
$$
Y_\eta=x+\eta b+\sqrt{2\eta a}\,G,
$$
where $x\in B$, $a>0$, and $b\in\mathbb R^d$ are fixed as $\eta\to0$. Its density is
$$
p_\eta(y)=(4\pi\eta a)^{-d/2}
\exp\left[-\frac{\|y-x-\eta b\|^2}{4\eta a}\right].
$$
For every $y\in D_+$, the triangle inequality gives
$\|y-x-\eta b\|\geq r_x-\eta\|b\|$. For sufficiently small $\eta$, this lower bound is positive. Since $D_+\subset\mathcal C$,
$$
\mathbb P(Y_\eta\in D_+)
\leq |\mathcal C|(4\pi\eta a)^{-d/2}
\exp\left[-\frac{(r_x-\eta\|b\|)^2}{4\eta a}\right].
$$
Taking logarithms and multiplying by $\eta$, the volume and Gaussian normalization terms tend to zero because $\eta\log\eta\to0$. Hence
$$
\limsup_{\eta\to0}\eta\log\mathbb P(Y_\eta\in D_+)
\leq-\frac{r_x^2}{4a}.
$$
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of distance, choose $y_\varepsilon\in D_+$ with $\|y_\varepsilon-x\|<r_x+\varepsilon$. Openness of $D_+$ provides a radius $0<s_\varepsilon<\varepsilon$ such that the ball $\mathcal{B}(y_\varepsilon,s_\varepsilon)$ lies in $D_+$. For every point $y$ of this ball,
$$
\|y-x-\eta b\|
\leq\|y-y_\varepsilon\|+\|y_\varepsilon-x\|+\eta\|b\|
<r_x+2\varepsilon+\eta\|b\|.
$$
Integrating the density over this ball therefore yields
$$
\mathbb P(Y_\eta\in D_+)
\geq |\mathcal{B}(y_\varepsilon,s_\varepsilon)|(4\pi\eta a)^{-d/2}
\exp\left[-\frac{(r_x+2\varepsilon+\eta\|b\|)^2}{4\eta a}\right].
$$
For fixed $\varepsilon$, the ball has positive volume independent of $\eta$. Taking $\liminf$ after multiplying the logarithm by $\eta$ gives
$$
\liminf_{\eta\to0}\eta\log\mathbb P(Y_\eta\in D_+)
\geq-\frac{(r_x+2\varepsilon)^2}{4a}.
$$
Now let $\varepsilon\rightarrow0$ and combine the two bounds to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof:bottleneck:gaussian:ldp}
\lim_{\eta\to0}\eta\log\mathbb P(Y_\eta\in D_+)
=-\frac{r_x^2}{4a}.
\end{equation}
For normalized RALMC, take $a=a_{\rm n}(x)$ and $b=-a_{\rm n}(x)\nabla U_0(x)$ in Equation~\eqref{eq:proof:bottleneck:gaussian:ldp}. For PLMC, take $a=1$ and $b=-\nabla U(x)$. The projection identities at the start of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:bottleneck:minorization} then give Equation~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:exact:rates}. Subtracting the two limits gives Equation~\eqref{eq:bottleneck:probability:ratio}. Since $r_x>0$, its right hand side is strictly positive if and only if $a_{\rm n}(x)>1$. The final uniform bound follows from $r_x\geq r$ and $a_{\rm n}(x)\geq\rho$.
<!-- end proof-51 -->
